From Innermost to Full Probabilistic Term Rewriting: Almost-Sure Termination, Complexity, and Modularity Thanks: Funded by the DFG Research Training Group 2236 UnRAVeL
Jan-Christoph Kassing ORCID:0009-0001-9972-2470, Jürgen Giesl ORCID:0000-0003-0283-8520

Abstract
. There are many evaluation strategies for term rewrite systems, but automatically proving termination or analyzing complexity is usually easiest for innermost rewriting. Several syntactic criteria exist when innermost termination implies (full) termination or when runtime complexity and innermost runtime complexity coincide. We adapt these criteria to the probabilistic setting, e.g., we show when it suffices to analyze almost-sure termination w.r.t. innermost rewriting in order to prove (full) almost-sure termination of probabilistic term rewrite systems. These criteria can be applied for both termination and complexity analysis in the probabilistic setting. We implemented and evaluated our new contributions in the tool AProVE . Moreover, we also use our new results to investigate the modularity of probabilistic termination properties.

1.
Introduction

Termination
and complexity analysis are among the main tasks in program verification, and
techniques and tools to analyze termination or complexity of
term rewrite systems (TRSs) automatically have been studied for decades.
While a direct application of classical reduction orderings is often too weak,
these orderings can be used successfully within the
dependency pair
(DP) framework
for termination
[
4
,
45
]
and for innermost runtime complexity
[
84
]
.
Moreover, the framework of
[
7
]
uses
weak

dependency pairs in order to analyze derivational and runtime complexity.
These frameworks allow for modular termination and complexity proofs
by decomposing the original problem into sub-problems which can
then be analyzed independently using different techniques.
Thus, DPs are used in essentially all current termination and complexity
tools for TRSs
(e.g.,
AProVE

[
39
]
,
MuTerm

[
42
]
,

NaTT

[
98
]
,

TcT

[
9
]
,

T
T
T
2

[
67
]
).
To allow certification of proofs with DPs, they have been formalized in
several proof assistants (e.g., in
Rocq
(formerly
Coq
)
[
22
,
16
]
,

Isabelle

[
94
]
, and recently together with the size-change
principle
[
71
,
91
,
82
]

in
PVS

[
1
,
75
]
), and there exist several corresponding certification tools
for termination and complexity proofs with DPs (e.g.,
CeTA

[
94
]
).

Probabilistic
programs are used to describe randomized
algorithms and probability distributions, with applications in many areas,
see, e.g.,
[
41
]
.
To use TRSs also for such programs,
probabilistic term rewrite systems
(PTRSs) were introduced in
[
15
,
14
,
3
]
.
In the probabilistic setting, there are several notions of “termination”.
For example, a program is
almost-surely terminating
(
\mathtt{AST}
)
if the probability for termination is
1
.
Another interesting property is
positive almost-sure termination
(
\mathtt{PAST}
)
[
89
,
14
]
, which means
that the expected length of every evaluation is finite.
Finally,
strong
or
bounded almost-sure termination

(
\mathtt{SAST}
)
[
33
,
3
]

requires that, for every
configuration
t
(e.g., for every term), there is a finite bound on the expected
lengths of all evaluations starting in
t
. Thus, if there is a start configuration

t
which non-deterministically leads to evaluations of arbitrary finite length, then the
program can be
\mathtt{PAST}
, but not
\mathtt{SAST}
.
In general,

\mathtt{SAST}
implies
\mathtt{PAST}
, and
\mathtt{PAST}
implies
\mathtt{AST}
,
but the converse directions do not hold.

We recently developed an adaption of the DP framework for
\mathtt{AST}

[
56
]
and an adaption for
innermost
\mathtt{AST}

[
54
,
52
]
(i.e.,
\mathtt{AST}
restricted to rewrite sequences where one only
evaluates at innermost positions), which allows us to benefit from a similar modularity
as in the non-probabilistic setting.
However, the DP framework for innermost
\mathtt{AST}
is
substantially more powerful than the one for
\mathtt{AST}
.
Indeed, also in the non-probabilistic setting,
innermost termination is usually substantially easier to prove than (full) termination,
i.e., termination w.r.t. any possible evaluation strategy,
see, e.g.,
[
4
,
45
]
.
The same holds for non-probabilistic complexity analysis, where the DP framework
of
[
84
]
is restricted to innermost rewriting and
the framework of
[
7
]

is considerably more powerful for innermost than for full rewriting.
To lift innermost termination and complexity proofs to full rewriting,
in the non-probabilistic setting there exist several sufficient criteria
which ensure that innermost termination implies full termination
[
43
]

and that innermost runtime complexity coincides with full runtime complexity
[
36
]
.

Up to now no such results were known in the probabilistic setting.
Our paper presents the first sufficient criteria for PTRSs which ensure that, e.g.,
\mathtt{AST}

coincides for full and innermost rewriting, and we also show similar results for
other rewrite strategies like
leftmost-innermost
rewriting.
We focus on criteria that can be checked automatically, so we can combine our results
with the DP framework for proving innermost
\mathtt{AST}
of PTRSs
[
54
,
52
]
.
In this way, we obtain a technique that (if applicable) can
prove
\mathtt{AST}
for
full
rewriting automatically
and is significantly more powerful than the corresponding DP framework for full
\mathtt{AST}

[
56
]
.

Our criteria also hold for
\mathtt{PAST}
,
\mathtt{SAST}
, and expected complexity.
Similar to
\mathtt{AST}
, very recently we developed an adaption of the DP framework to prove innermost
\mathtt{SAST}

and upper bounds on the innermost expected runtime complexity
[
66
]
.
Again, we can use our results on the relation between innermost and full rewriting to
apply this adaption of the DP framework for arbitrary evaluation strategies.
A corresponding evaluation can be found in
[
66
]
.
Except for applying polynomial or matrix orderings directly on the whole
PTRS
[
3
]
, currently there do not
exist any other automatic techniques for
\mathtt{PAST}
,
\mathtt{SAST}
, or expected
complexity of PTRSs.

As a corollary of our results in the probabilistic setting,
we also develop the first results relating derivational and innermost
derivational complexity for ordinary non-probabilistic TRSs.
The difference between derivational and runtime complexity is that
runtime complexity only considers start terms where a defined function symbol (i.e., an
“algorithm”)
is applied
to arguments built with constructor symbols (i.e., to “data”),
while derivational complexity allows arbitrary start terms.

There exist numerous results on the
modularity of termination, confluence, and completeness of TRSs
in the non-probabilistic setting, see, e.g.,
[
43
,
44
,
92
,
93
]
.
Based on our novel criteria,
we develop the first modularity
results for probabilistic termination w.r.t. different evaluation strategies,
i.e.,
we investigate whether
\mathtt{AST}
,
\mathtt{PAST}
, or
\mathtt{SAST}
are preserved
for unions of PTRSs.
Additionally, we also study preservation of
\mathtt{AST}
,
\mathtt{PAST}
, or
\mathtt{SAST}

under signature extensions, which can be seen as a special case of modularity, i.e., a specific union of PTRSs,
where the second PTRS only contains trivially terminating rewrite rules over the new signature.
We show that while
\mathtt{AST}
and
\mathtt{SAST}
are preserved under signature extensions, this does not hold for
\mathtt{PAST}
,
implying that any sound and complete proof technique for
\mathtt{PAST}
of PTRSs
has to take the specific signature into account.
Related to these results, we show that
\mathtt{PAST}
and
\mathtt{SAST}
coincide
for a broad class of PTRSs.
For example, if the signature contains at
least one function symbol of arity greater than
1
,
then there is no difference between

\mathtt{PAST}
and
\mathtt{SAST}
for finite PTRSs.

Structure

We start with preliminaries on term rewriting in
Section

2
.
Then we recapitulate PTRSs based on
[
14
,
29
,
3
,
32
,
54
]

in
Section

3
.
In
Section

4
we show that the properties of
[
43
]

that ensure equivalence of innermost and full termination do not suffice in the probabilistic setting and extend them accordingly.
In particular, we show that innermost and full
\mathtt{AST}
coincide for PTRSs that are
non-overlapping and linear.
This result also holds for
\mathtt{PAST}
,
\mathtt{SAST}
, and the expected runtime complexity, as well as for strategies like
leftmost-innermost evaluation.
In
Section

5
we try to weaken the linearity
requirement in order to prove
full
\mathtt{AST}
for larger classes of PTRSs.
The implementation of our criteria in the tool
AProVE
is evaluated in
Section

6
.
Afterwards, in
Section

7
we analyze the
modularity of all these (full and innermost)
termination properties for PTRSs.
We discuss related work on the
verification of probabilistic programs in
Section

8
.
Finally, we conclude in
Section

9
and
refer to App.
A
for all missing proofs.

in the main part of the paper and present the corresponding full technical proofs in
App.
A
.

from

.

2.
Preliminaries

For any relation
\to\;\subseteq A\times A
on some set
A
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we define
\to^{n}
as

\to^{0}\;=\;\{(a,a)\mid a\in A\}
and
\to^{n+1}\;=\;\to^{n}\circ\to
,
where “
\circ
” denotes composition of relations, and define
\to^{*}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\to^{n}
,
i.e.,
\to^{*}
is the
reflexive and transitive closure
of
\to
.
Let
\mathtt{NF}_{\to}
denote the set of all elements that are in
normal form
w.r.t.
\to
,
i.e., for all
a\in\mathtt{NF}_{\to}
there is no
b\in A
with
a\to b
.

We assume familiarity with term rewriting
[
19
]
, but recapitulate
the notions that are needed for this work.
We write
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma,\mathcal{V})
for the set of all
terms
over a (possibly infinite) countable
set of
function symbols

\Sigma=\biguplus_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\Sigma_{k}
and a (possibly infinite) countable set of
variables

\mathcal{V}
,
and
\mathcal{T}
if the specific sets
\Sigma
and
\mathcal{V}
are irrelevant or clear from the context.
To be precise,
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma,\mathcal{V})
is the smallest set with
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathcal{T}(\Sigma,\mathcal{V})
,
and if
f\in\Sigma_{k}
and
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma,\mathcal{V})
then
f(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma,\mathcal{V})
.
We say that a function symbol has
arity

k\in\mathbb{N}
if
f\in\Sigma_{k}
,
i.e., the arity denotes how many arguments a function symbol takes.
A
substitution
is a function
\sigma:\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{T}
with
\sigma(x)=x
for all but finitely many
x\in\mathcal{V}
,
and we often write
x\sigma
instead of
\sigma(x)
.
Substitutions homomorphically extend to terms: If
t=f(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})\in\mathcal{T}
then
t\sigma=f(t_{1}\sigma,\dots,t_{k}\sigma)
.
For a term
t\in\mathcal{T}
, the set of
positions

\mathrm{Pos}(t)

is the smallest subset of
\mathbb{N}^{*}
satisfying
\varepsilon\in\mathrm{Pos}(t)
,
and if
t=f(t_{1},\dots,t_{k})
then for all
1\leq i\leq k
and all
\pi\in\mathrm{Pos}(t_{i})
we have
i.\pi\in\mathrm{Pos}(t)
.
If
\pi\in\mathrm{Pos}(t)
then
t|_{\pi}
denotes the subterm starting at position
\pi

and
t[r]_{\pi}
denotes the term that results from replacing the subterm
t|_{\pi}
at position
\pi
with the term
r\in\mathcal{T}
.
A
context

C
is a term containing
exactly one occurrence of
a special constant symbol
\square
,
denoting a hole.
The constant
\square
is used to indicate where one can insert a term,
i.e., if
\square
is at position
\pi
of
C
, then
C[t]
is a shorthand notation for
C[t]_{\pi}
.

A
rewrite rule

\ell\to r
is a pair of terms
\ell,r\in\mathcal{T}

such that
\mathcal{V}(r)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\ell)
and
\ell\notin\mathcal{V}
,
where
\mathcal{V}(t)
denotes the set of all variables occurring in
t\in\mathcal{T}
.
A
term rewrite system
(TRS) is a (possibly infinite) countable set of rewrite rules.
As an example, consider the TRS
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{d}}
that doubles a natural number (represented by the terms
\mathsf{s}
and
\mathsf{0}
)
with the rewrite rules
\mathsf{d}(\mathsf{s}(x))\to\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{d}(x)))
and
\mathsf{d}(\mathsf{0})\to\mathsf{0}
.
A TRS
\mathcal{R}
induces a
rewrite relation

{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
on terms where
s\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}t
holds if there is a position
\pi\in\mathrm{Pos}(s)
,
a rule
\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}
, and a substitution
\sigma
such that
s|_{\pi}=\ell\sigma
and
t=s[r\sigma]_{\pi}
.
Here,
\mathbf{f}
stands for ‘‘full rewriting’’
2
2

2

In the literature,
one usually simply writes
\to_{\mathcal{R}}
instead.
We use
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}
here to clearly indicate the corresponding rewrite strategy.

as we did not fix any specific strategy yet.
A rewrite step
s\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}t
is an
innermost
rewrite step (denoted
s\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}t
) if
all proper subterms of the used
redex

\ell\sigma
are in normal form w.r.t.
\mathcal{R}
, i.e.,
the proper subterms of
\ell\sigma

do not contain redexes themselves and thus, they cannot be reduced with
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}
.
For example, we have
\mathsf{d}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{d}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0}))))\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{d}}}}\mathsf{d}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{d}(\mathsf{0})))))
.
Let
\mathtt{NF}_{\mathcal{R}}
denote the set of all terms that are in normal form w.r.t.
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}
.

Let
<
be the
prefix ordering
on positions and let
\leq
be its reflexive closure.
Two positions
\tau
and
\pi
are
parallel
if both
\tau\not\leq\pi
and
\pi\not\leq\tau
hold.
For two parallel positions
\tau
and
\pi
we define

\tau\prec\pi
if we have
i<j
for the unique
i,j
such that
\chi.i\leq\tau
and
\chi.j\leq\pi
,
where
\chi
is the longest common prefix of
\tau
and
\pi
.
An innermost rewrite step
s\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}t
at position
\pi
is

leftmost
(denoted
s\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}t
) if
s
does not contain
any redex at a position

\tau
with
\tau\prec\pi
.

In this paper, we will consider innermost rewriting (
\mathbf{i}
), leftmost-innermost rewriting (
\mathbf{li}
),
and full rewriting (
\mathbf{f}
).
Since every leftmost-innermost rewrite step is an innermost rewrite step,
and every innermost rewrite step is a step w.r.t. full rewriting, one directly obtains

\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\;\subseteq\;\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\;\subseteq\;\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
for every TRS
\mathcal{R}
.
Let
\mathbb{S}=\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{i},\mathbf{li}\}
be the set of these three
rewrite strategies.

2.1.
Termination

Let
\to\;\subseteq\;\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
be a relation on terms.
We call
\to

strongly normalizing

or
\mathtt{SN}_{\to}
for short if
\to
is well founded. A TRS

\mathcal{R}
is
terminating
if we have
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
,
and
\mathcal{R}
is

innermost terminating
or
leftmost-innermost terminating

if we have
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
or
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
, respectively.
If every term
t\in\mathcal{T}
has a normal form w.r.t.
\to

(i.e., we have
t\to^{*}t^{\prime}
where
t^{\prime}\in\mathtt{NF}_{\to}
)
then we call
\to

weakly normalizing
(denoted by
\mathtt{WN}_{\to}
).

Two terms
s,t\in\mathcal{T}
are
joinable
via
\mathcal{R}

if there exists a
w\in\mathcal{T}
such that
s\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}^{*}w\mathrel{\prescript{*}{\mathcal{R}}{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\>\mathbf{f}$}}}{{\smash{\leftarrow}}}}}}}t
.
Two rules
\ell_{1}\to r_{1},\ell_{2}\to r_{2}\in\mathcal{R}
with renamed variables such that

\mathcal{V}(\ell_{1})\cap\mathcal{V}(\ell_{2})=\varnothing
are
overlapping

if there exists a non-variable position
\pi
of
\ell_{1}
such that
\ell_{1}|_{\pi}
and

\ell_{2}
are unifiable, i.e., there exists a substitution
\sigma
such that
\ell_{1}|_{\pi}\sigma=\ell_{2}\sigma
.
If
(\ell_{1}\to r_{1})=(\ell_{2}\to r_{2})
, then we require that
\pi\neq\varepsilon
.

\mathcal{R}
is
non-overlapping
(NO) if it has no overlapping rules.
As an example, the TRS
\mathcal{R}_{\mathsf{d}}
is non-overlapping.
A TRS is
left-linear
(LL) (
right-linear
, RL) if
every variable occurs at most once in the left-hand side (right-hand side) of a rule.
A TRS is
linear
if it is both left- and right-linear.
A TRS is
non-erasing
(NE)
if in every rule,
all variables of the left-hand side also occur
in the right-hand side.

Next, we recapitulate the relations
between
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
,
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
,
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
, and
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
in the non-probabilistic
setting.
Obviously, the stronger notion always implies the weaker one, e.g.,
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
implies
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

and
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
implies
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

since
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\;\subseteq\;\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
.
The interesting question is for which classes of TRSs the weaker and the stronger notion
are equivalent.
3
3

3

Note that to this end, we do not have to consider

\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
. The reason is that
when
analyzing under which conditions

\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
implies
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
, we also know that under these conditions we have
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\Longrightarrow\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

for all
s\in\mathbb{S}
, since
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\Longrightarrow\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\Longrightarrow\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
hold.
Moreover, we have

\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\Longrightarrow\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
for all
s\in\mathbb{S}
.

We start with the relation between
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
.

Counterexample
\thethm

(Toyama’s Counterexample
[
92
]
)
.

For the TRS
\mathcal{R}_{1}
with the rules
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},x)\to\mathsf{f}(x,x,x)
,
\mathsf{g}\to\mathsf{a}
, and
\mathsf{g}\to\mathsf{b}
,
we do not have
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}}
due to the infinite rewrite sequence
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{g})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{g})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\ldots

But the only innermost rewrite sequences starting with
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{g})
are

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{g})\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{a})\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a})
and

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{g})\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b})\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{b})
,
i.e., both of them reach normal forms in the end.
Thus,
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}}
holds as
we have to rewrite the inner
\mathsf{g}
before we can use the
\mathsf{f}
-rule.

The first property known to ensure equivalence of
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
is orthogonality,
which was already shown in
[
87
]
.
A TRS is
orthogonal
(OR) if it is non-overlapping and left-linear.

Theorem 1

(From
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
to
{\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
(1),
[
87
]
)
.

If a TRS
\mathcal{R}
is OR, then:

\displaystyle\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\!

Then, in
[
43
]
, it was shown that one can remove the requirement
of left-linearity.

Theorem 2

(From
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
to
{\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
(2),
[
43
]
)
.

If a TRS
\mathcal{R}
is NO, then:

\displaystyle\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\!

Moreover,
[
43
]
refined

Theorem

2
further.
A TRS
\mathcal{R}
is an
overlay system
(OS) if
its rules may only overlap at the root position, i.e.,

\pi=\varepsilon
.
For instance,
\mathcal{R}_{1}
from Counterex.
2.1
is an overlay system.
Furthermore, a TRS is
locally confluent
(or
weakly Church-Rosser
, abbreviated WCR)
if for all terms
s,t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathcal{T}
such that
t_{1}\mathrel{\prescript{}{\mathcal{R}}{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\>\mathbf{f}$}}}{{\smash{\leftarrow}}}}}}}s\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}t_{2}
the terms
t_{1}
and
t_{2}
are joinable.

\mathcal{R}_{1}
is
not
WCR, as we have
\mathsf{a}\mathrel{\prescript{}{\mathcal{R}_{1}}{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\>\mathbf{f}$}}}{{\smash{\leftarrow}}}}}}}\mathsf{g}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{1}}\mathsf{b}
, but
\mathsf{a}
and
\mathsf{b}
are not joinable.
If a TRS has both of these properties, then
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
are again equivalent.

Theorem 3

(From
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
to
{\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
(3),
[
43
]
)
.

If a TRS
\mathcal{R}
is OS and WCR, then:

\displaystyle\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\!

Theorem

3
is stronger than
Theorem

2

as every non-overlapping TRS is a locally confluent overlay
system
[
51
]
.

Next, we recapitulate the results on the
relation between
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
.

Counterexample 4
.

Consider the TRS
\mathcal{R}_{2}
with the rules
\mathsf{f}(x)\to\mathsf{b}
and
\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})
.
We do not have
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}}
since we can always rewrite the inner
\mathsf{a}
to get

\mathsf{a}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}))\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{2}}\ldots
.
On the other hand,
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
holds since we can also rewrite the outer
\mathsf{f}(\ldots)

before we use the
\mathsf{a}
-rule twice, resulting in the term
\mathsf{b}
, which is a normal form.
For the TRS
\mathcal{R}_{3}
with the rules
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})\to\mathsf{b}
and
\mathsf{a}\to\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})
, the situation is similar.

The TRS
\mathcal{R}_{2}
from Counterex.
4
is erasing and

\mathcal{R}_{3}
is overlapping.
For TRSs with neither of those two properties,
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
are equivalent.

Theorem 5

(From
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
to
{\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}}
,
[
43
]
)
.

If a TRS
\mathcal{R}
is NO and NE, then:

\displaystyle\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\!

Finally, we look at the relation between rewrite strategies that use an ordering
for parallel redexes like leftmost-innermost rewriting compared to innermost rewriting.
It turns out that such an ordering does not interfere with termination at all.

Theorem 6

(From
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
to
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
,
[
65
]
)
.

For all TRSs
\mathcal{R}
we have:

\displaystyle\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

\displaystyle\Longleftrightarrow\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\!

The relations between the different termination properties for non-probabilistic TRSs (given in

Theorem

3
,
5
,
and
6
) are summarized in
Figure

1
.

\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
\mathtt{WN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
OS
+
WCR
NO
+
NE

Figure 1
.
Relations between the different termination properties for TRSs

2.2.
Complexity

Next, we recapitulate known results regarding the complexity of TRSs under different
rewrite strategies.
There are two standard notions of complexity used in term rewriting:
derivational
and
runtime
complexity

[
49
,
50
]
.
For any
M\subseteq\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
,
\sup M
denotes the least upper bound of
M
,
where
\sup\varnothing=0
.
For a relation
{\to}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
,
the
derivation height

\operatorname{dh}_{\to}:\mathcal{T}\to\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}

of a term
t\in\mathcal{T}

is
\operatorname{dh}_{\to}(t)=\sup\{m\mid\exists t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}:t\to^{m}t^{\prime}\}
,
i.e., the length of the longest
\to
-rewrite sequence starting with
t
.
Then, the
derivational complexity

\operatorname{dc}_{\to}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
of
\to
is defined as

\operatorname{dc}_{\to}(n)=\sup\{\operatorname{dh}_{\to}(t)\mid t\in\mathcal{T},|t|\leq n\}
.
Here, the
size

|t|
of a term
t
is the number of occurrences of
function symbols and variables in
t
, i.e.,
we have
|x|=1
for
x\in\mathcal{V}
and
|f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})|=1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}|t_{i}|
.
So
\operatorname{dc}_{\to}(n)
denotes the length of the longest
\to
-rewrite
sequence starting with an arbitrary term of at most size
n
.
For a TRS
\mathcal{R}
and a strategy
s\in\mathbb{S}
, the
derivational complexity
of
\mathcal{R}
w.r.t.
s

is
\operatorname{dc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
.

In contrast, for
runtime complexity
one restricts the start terms to be
basic
.
For a TRS
\mathcal{R}
, we decompose its signature
\Sigma=\Sigma_{C}\uplus\Sigma_{D}
such
that
f\in\Sigma_{D}
if
f=\operatorname{root}(\ell)
for some rule
\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}
.
The symbols in
\Sigma_{C}
and
\Sigma_{D}
are called
constructors
and

defined symbols
, respectively.
A term
t\in\mathcal{T}
is
basic
if
t=f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k})
such that
f\in\Sigma_{D}

and
t_{i}\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma_{C},\mathcal{V})
for all
1\leq i\leq k
, and the set of all basic terms is denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
.
The
runtime complexity
of a relation
{\to}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\mathcal{T}
w.r.t. a TRS
\mathcal{R}
maps any
n\in\mathbb{N}

to the length of the longest
\to
-rewrite sequence starting with a basic term
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
with
|t|\leq n
.
So
\operatorname{rc}_{\to,\mathcal{R}}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
is defined as

\operatorname{rc}_{\to,\mathcal{R}}(n)=\sup\{\operatorname{dh}_{\to}(t)\mid t\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}},|t|\leq n\}
, where

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
are the basic terms w.r.t.
\mathcal{R}
.
For a TRS
\mathcal{R}
and a strategy
s\in\mathbb{S}
, the
runtime complexity
of
\mathcal{R}
w.r.t.
s

is
\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}},\mathcal{R}}
, where we often omit the additional index
\mathcal{R}
for
readability, i.e.,
\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}},\mathcal{R}}=\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
.

We are not aware of any non-trivial
classes of TRSs where innermost and full derivational complexity coincide,
i.e., where we have
\operatorname{dc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}(n)=\operatorname{dc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}(n)
for
all
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
all TRSs
\mathcal{R}
from that class.
However, for runtime complexity, there exist sufficient criteria for TRSs

\mathcal{R}
which imply

\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}=\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

[
36
]
.
To relate innermost and full runtime complexity, it does not suffice to require
that the rules are non-overlapping, but we also have to make sure that we cannot
duplicate redexes during an evaluation, as shown by the following counterexample from
[
36
]
.

Counterexample 7
.

Let
\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0})
abbreviate
\underbrace{\mathsf{s}(\ldots\mathsf{s}(}_{n\,\text{times}}\!\mathsf{0})\ldots)

and let
\mathcal{R}_{4}
consist of the rules:

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0},y)

\displaystyle\to y

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}(x),y)

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{f}(x,\mathsf{node}(y,y))\!

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}(x)

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{f}(x,\mathsf{a})

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}

\displaystyle\to\mathsf{b}\!

Note that
\mathcal{R}_{4}
is non-overlapping.
For the basic term
\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}))
of size
n+2
we have
\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}))\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}_{4}}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}),\mathsf{a})
.
Next, one could apply the second
\mathsf{f}
-rule repeatedly and
obtain a term corresponding to a full binary tree of height
n
whose
exponentially many leaves all correspond to the symbol
\mathsf{a}
.
Finally, these leaves can all be reduced to
\mathsf{b}
in
2^{n}
steps,
hence
\operatorname{rc}^{\mathcal{R}_{4}}_{\mathbf{f}}(n)\in\Theta(2^{n})
.
On the other hand, any basic term of size
n
only leads to innermost rewrite sequences of length
\mathcal{O}(n)
,
as for example in
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}),\mathsf{a})
we have to rewrite the inner
\mathsf{a}
before we
are able to duplicate it.
Thus, we obtain
\operatorname{rc}^{\mathcal{R}_{4}}_{\mathbf{i}}(n)\in\Theta(n)
.

The property that no redexes are duplicated during a rewrite sequence
that starts with a basic term is called
spareness

[
36
]
.
For a TRS
\mathcal{R}
, a rewrite step using the rule
\ell\to r\in\mathcal{R}
and the substitution

\sigma
is
spare

if
\sigma(x)\in\mathtt{NF}_{\mathcal{R}}
for every
x\in\mathcal{V}
that occurs more than once in
r
.
A
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}
-rewrite sequence is spare if each of its
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}
-steps is spare.

\mathcal{R}
is spare (SP) if each
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}
-rewrite sequence that starts with a basic term
t\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
is spare.
In general, it is undecidable whether a TRS is spare.
However, there exist computable sufficient conditions for
spareness, see
[
36
]
.

Similar to the results regarding termination, for the equivalence of

\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

it again suffices to require
overlay systems instead of non-overlapping TRSs.
However, while we have to require spareness, in contrast to
Theorem

3

we do not need local confluence.

Theorem 8

(From
\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
to
\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
,
[
36
]
)
.

If a TRS
\mathcal{R}
is OS and SP, then:

\displaystyle\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}\!

Counterex.
7
also works for derivational complexity,
showing that duplication of redexes needs to be prohibited
for equivalence of
\operatorname{dc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
and
\operatorname{dc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
as well.
However, note that
spareness
requires basic start terms,
so an analogous theorem for derivational complexity would have to require a stronger
property,
e.g., right-linearity of
\mathcal{R}
.
We will present such a result as a corollary of our analysis of probabilistic rewriting
in
Section

4
(
Corollary

22
).
Finally,
the relation between the runtime complexities for
rewrite strategies
that use an ordering for parallel redexes has not been studied so far.
Here, our probabilistic analysis will also yield a corresponding
corollary (
Corollary

28
).

3.
Probabilistic Term Rewriting

In
Section

3.1
, we define
probabilistic
TRSs

[
14
,
3
,
54
]
.
In
Section

3.2
, we recapitulate
notions of termination
in the probabilistic setting, like almost-sure termination (
\mathtt{AST}
), positive almost-sure termination (
\mathtt{PAST}
),
strong almost-sure termination (
\mathtt{SAST}
), and we introduce a novel definition
of
expected derivational/runtime complexity
.
Then in
Section

3.3
, we
present new results on the
relation between
\mathtt{PAST}
and
\mathtt{SAST}
.

3.1.
Probabilistic Term Rewriting

In contrast to TRSs, a PTRS has finite
4
4

4

The restriction to

finite
multi-distributions allows us to simplify the handling of PTRSs in the proofs. However, we conjecture that most of our results also hold for PTRSs with infinite countable multi-distributions.

multi-distributions on the right-hand sides of its rewrite rules.
5
5

5

A different form of probabilistic rewrite rules was proposed in
PMaude

[
8
]
, where numerical extra variables in right-hand sides of rules are instantiated according to a probability distribution.

A finite
multi-distribution

\mu
on a set
A\neq\varnothing
is a finite multiset of pairs
(p:a)
, where
0<p\leq 1
is a probability and
a\in A
, such that
\sum_{(p:a)\in\mu}p=1
.

\operatorname{FDist}(A)
is the set of all finite multi-distributions on
A
.
For
\mu\in\operatorname{FDist}(A)
, its
support
is the multiset
\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)=\{a\mid(p:a)\in\mu
for some
p\}
.
A
probabilistic rewrite rule
is a pair
(\ell\to\mu)\in\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
such that
\ell\not\in\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{V}(r)\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\ell)
for every
r\in\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)
.
A
probabilistic TRS
(PTRS) is a (possibly infinite) countable set
\mathcal{P}
of probabilistic rewrite rules.
Similar to TRSs, the PTRS
\mathcal{P}
induces a
rewrite relation

{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
where
s\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\{p_{1}:t_{1},\ldots,p_{k}:t_{k}\}
if there is a position
\pi
, a rule
\ell\to\{p_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,p_{k}:r_{k}\}\in\mathcal{P}
, and a substitution
\sigma
such that
s|_{\pi}=\ell\sigma
and
t_{j}=s[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
.
We call
s\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\mu
an
innermost
rewrite step (denoted
s\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\mu
)
if all proper subterms of the used redex
\ell\sigma
are in normal form w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.
We have
s\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\mu
if the rewrite step
s\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\mu
at position
\pi
is
leftmost (i.e., there is no redex at a position
\tau
with
\tau\prec\pi
).
For example, the PTRS
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}
with the only rule
\mathsf{g}\to\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g}),\;\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{0}\}
corresponds to a symmetric
random walk on the number of
\mathsf{g}
-symbols in a term.

Many properties of TRSs from
Section

2
can be lifted to PTRSs in a
straightforward way:
A PTRS
\mathcal{P}
is right-linear (non-erasing) iff the TRS

\{\ell\to r\mid\ell\to\mu\in\mathcal{P},r\in\operatorname{Supp}(\mu)\}

has the respective property.
Moreover, all properties that just consider the left-hand sides, e.g., normal forms, left-linearity,
being
non-overlapping, orthogonality, and being an overlay system,
can be lifted to PTRSs directly as well, since their rules again only have a single
left-hand side.

3.2.
Probabilistic Notions of Termination

Next, we introduce the different notions of probabilistic termination.
In this section, we regard an arbitrary
probabilistic (term) relation

\to\;\subseteq\;\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})

and let
\mathtt{NF}_{\to}
again be the set of all normal forms for
\to
.
As in
[
29
,
3
,
32
,
54
]
, we

lift

\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
to a rewrite relation
\rightrightarrows\;\subseteq\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
between multi-distributions in order to track all probabilistic rewrite sequences (up to non-determinism) at once.
For any
0<p\leq 1
and any
\mu\in\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
, let
p\cdot\mu=\{(p\cdot q:a)\mid(q:a)\in\mu\}
.

{defi}

[Lifting]
The
lifting

{\rightrightarrows}\subseteq\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
of a relation
{\to}\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
is the smallest relation with:

\bullet

If
t\in\mathcal{T}
is in normal form w.r.t.
\rightarrow
, then
\{1:t\}\rightrightarrows\{1:t\}
.

\bullet

If
t\to\mu
, then
\{1:t\}\rightrightarrows\mu
.

\bullet

If for all
1\leq j\leq k
there are
\mu_{j},\nu_{j}\in\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
with
\mu_{j}\rightrightarrows\nu_{j}
and
0<p_{j}\leq 1
with
\sum_{1\leq j\leq k}p_{j}=1
, then
\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}p_{j}\cdot\mu_{j}\rightrightarrows\bigcup_{1\leq j\leq k}p_{j}\cdot\nu_{j}
.

For a PTRS
\mathcal{P}
, we write
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
, and
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
for
the liftings of
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
,
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
, and
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
, respectively.

{exa}

We obtain the following
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
-rewrite sequence (which is also a

\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
-rewrite sequence, but not a
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
-rewrite sequence).

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\{1:\mathsf{g}\}\\
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}&\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g}),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{0}\}\\
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}&\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g}),\mathsf{g}),\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{g}),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{0}\}\\
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}&\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g}),\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g})),\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g}),\mathsf{0}),\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g})),\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{0}),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{0}\}\end{array}

Another way to track all possible rewrite sequences with their corresponding probabilities
is to lift
\to
to
rewrite sequence trees (RSTs)

[
52
]
.
The nodes
v
of an
\to
-RST are labeled by pairs
(p_{v}:t_{v})
of a
probability
p_{v}
and a term
t_{v}
, where
the root is always labeled with the probability
1
.
For each node
v
with the successors
w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}
, the edge relation represents a
step with the relation
\to
,
i.e.,
t_{v}\to\{\tfrac{p_{w_{1}}}{p_{v}}:t_{w_{1}},\ldots,\tfrac{p_{w_{k}}}{p_{v}}:t_{w_{k}}\}
.
For an
\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
,
let
N^{\mathfrak{T}}
denote the set of nodes and
\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}
denote the set of leaves.
We say that
\mathfrak{T}
is
fully evaluated
if for every
v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}

the corresponding term
t_{v}
is a normal form w.r.t.
\to
, i.e.,
t_{v}\in\mathtt{NF}_{\to}
.
In
Figure

2
one can see the
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}
-RST for the
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
-rewrite sequence from
Section

3.2
.
Note that the normal forms remain in each multi-distribution of a

\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
-rewrite sequence,
but they are leaves of the corresponding
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}
-RST.

\mu_{0}:
1
\mathsf{g}
\mu_{1}:
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g})
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
\mathsf{0}
\quad\mathtt{NF}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}
\mu_{2}:
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g}),\mathsf{g})
\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}
\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{g})
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

Figure 2
.
Corresponding RST for the
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}
-rewrite sequence in
Section

3.2
.

To express the concept of almost-sure termination, one has to determine the probability for normal forms in a multi-distribution.

{defi}

[
|\mu|_{\to}
,
|\mu|_{\mathcal{P}}
]
Let
\mu\in\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
.
For a relation
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
, let
|\mu|_{\to}=\sum_{(p:t)\in\mu,t\in\mathtt{NF}_{\to}}p
,
and for a PTRS
\mathcal{P}
, let
|\mu|_{\mathcal{P}}=\sum_{(p:t)\in\mu,t\in\mathtt{NF}_{\mathcal{P}}}p
.

{exa}

Consider
\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g}),\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g})),\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g}),\mathsf{0}),\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g})),\linebreak\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{0}),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{0}\}=\mu
from
Section

3.2
.
Then
|\mu|_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}=\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}+\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}=\nicefrac{{5}}{{8}}
,
since
\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{0})
and
\mathsf{0}
are both normal forms w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}
.

{defi}

[Almost-Sure Termination
[
3
]
]
Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})

and
\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an infinite

\rightrightarrows
-rewrite
sequence, i.e.,
\mu_{n}\rightrightarrows\mu_{n+1}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. We say that

\vec{\mu}

converges with probability

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}|_{\to}
.
The relation
\to
is
almost-surely terminating
(denoted
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}
)
if
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}|_{\to}=1

holds for every infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
We say that
\to
is
weakly
\mathtt{AST}
(denoted
\mathtt{wAST}_{\to}
) if for every term

t
there exists an infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

with
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}|_{\to}=1
and
\mu_{0}=\{1:t\}
.

For the definition of
\mathtt{wAST}
recall that
by
Section

3.2
every term (even normal forms)
can start infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequences,
as we keep normal forms in
\rightrightarrows
-steps.

Equivalently, one can also define
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}
(and
\mathtt{wAST}_{\to}
) via
\to
-RSTs.
For any
\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
we define its

convergence probability

|\mathfrak{T}|=\sum_{v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}p_{v}
.
Then
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}
holds iff for all
\to
-RSTs
\mathfrak{T}
we have
|\mathfrak{T}|=1
.
Moreover,
\mathtt{wAST}_{\to}
holds iff for every term
t
there exists a fully evaluated
\to
-RST

\mathfrak{T}
whose root is labeled with
(1:t)
such that
|\mathfrak{T}|=1
.

{exa}

For every infinite extension
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
-rewrite sequence in

Section

3.2
, we have
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}|_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}=1
. Indeed, we have
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}

and thus also
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
,
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
, and
\mathtt{wAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
.

Next, we define
positive
almost-sure termination, which
considers the
expected derivation length

\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\mu})
of a rewrite sequence
\vec{\mu}
,
i.e., the expected number of steps until one reaches a normal form.
For positive almost-sure termination, we require that the expected derivation length
of every possible rewrite sequence is finite.
In the following definition,
(1-|\mu_{n}|_{\to})
is the probability of terms
that are
not
in normal form w.r.t.
\to
after the
n
-th step.

{defi}

[Positive Almost-Sure Termination,
\operatorname{edl}

[
3
]
]
Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
and
\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an infinite

\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence.
By
\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\mu})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-|\mu_{n}|_{\to})

we denote the
expected derivation length
of
\vec{\mu}
.
The relation
\to
is
positively almost-surely terminating
(denoted
\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
)
if
\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\mu})
is finite for every infinite

\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence
\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

starting with a single term, i.e.,
\mu_{0}=\{1:t\}
with
t\in\mathcal{T}
.
We say that
\to
is
weakly
\mathtt{PAST}
(denoted
\mathtt{wPAST}_{\to}
) if for every
term
t
there exists an infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite
sequence
\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\mu})
is finite and
\mu_{0}=\{1:t\}
.

In terms of
\to
-RSTs, we define the
expected derivation length
of an

\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
to be
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})=\sum_{v\in N^{\mathfrak{T}}\setminus\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}p_{v}
.
Then, we have
\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
iff
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
is finite
for every
\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
.
Similarly, we have
\mathtt{wPAST}_{\to}
iff for every term
t
there exists a fully evaluated
\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}

whose root is labeled with
(1:t)
such that
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
is finite.

Remark 9
.

For every
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence
\vec{\mu}
that converges with probability 1,
we also have
\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\mu})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-|\mu_{n}|_{\to})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n\cdot(|\mu_{n}|_{\to}-|\mu_{n-1}|_{\to})
,
where
|\mu_{n}|_{\to}-|\mu_{n-1}|_{\to}
denotes the probability that we reach a normal form in the
n
-th step.
This is due to the correspondence between a probability mass
function
f:\mathbb{N}\to[0,1]
and its distribution function
F:\mathbb{N}\to[0,1]
w.r.t. the expected value, namely
\mathbb{E}(f)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n\cdot f(n)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-F(n))
.
With
f_{\vec{\mu}}(0)=|\mu_{0}|_{\to}
and
f_{\vec{\mu}}(n)=|\mu_{n}|_{\to}-|\mu_{n-1}|_{\to}
for all
n>0
, we get
F_{\vec{\mu}}(n)=|\mu_{n}|_{\to}
, and
hence we obtain the above equation for
\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\mu})
.
Note that
f_{\vec{\mu}}
is only a probability mass function if
\lim_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}|_{\to}=1
,
because then
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}f_{\vec{\mu}}(n)=|\mu_{0}|_{\to}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(|\mu_{n}|_{\to}-|\mu_{n-1}|_{\to})=\lim_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}|_{\to}=1
.

It is well known that
\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
implies
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}
, but not vice versa.

{exa}

For every infinite extension
\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of the
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
-rewrite sequence in

Section

3.2
, the expected derivation length

\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\mu})
is infinite, hence
\mathtt{wPAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
does not hold, and

\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
,
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
, or

\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{rw}}}}
do not hold either.

Next, we define
strong almost-sure termination

[
33
,
3
]
,
which is even stricter than
\mathtt{PAST}
in case of non-determinism.
It requires a finite bound on the expected derivation lengths of all rewrite
sequences with the same start term.
For a term
t\in\mathcal{T}
, the
expected derivation height

\operatorname{edh}_{\to}(t)

considers all
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!}
-rewrite sequences that start with
\{1:t\}
.

{defi}

[Strong Almost-Sure Termination,
\operatorname{edh}

[
3
]
]
For every relation
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
we define
the
expected derivation height
of a term
t\in\mathcal{T}
by

\operatorname{edh}_{\to}(t)=\sup\{\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\mu})\mid\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\text{ is a
$\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!}$-rewrite sequence with }\mu_{0}=\{1:t\}\}
.
We say that
\to
is
strongly almost-surely terminating
(
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
)
if
\operatorname{edh}_{\to}(t)
is finite for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
.

In terms of
\to
-RSTs, we have
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
iff

\sup\{\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\mid\mathfrak{T}
is an
\to
-RST whose root is labeled with
(1:t)\}

is finite for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
.

Note that in contrast to
\mathtt{wAST}
and
\mathtt{wPAST}
, we did not define any notion of
weak

\mathtt{SAST}
. The
reason is that the definition of weak forms of termination always only requires the

existence
of some suitable rewrite sequence. But the definition of strong almost-sure termination
imposes a requirement on
all
rewrite sequences. Thus, it is not clear how to
obtain a useful definition for “weak
\mathtt{SAST}
” that differs from
\mathtt{wPAST}
.

\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
are already defined
in terms of the expected derivation length and height,
but they consider arbitrary start terms of arbitrary size.
As in the non-probabilistic setting, one can also regard
a function that maps the size of the start term to
the expected derivation length,
leading to the novel notion of the
expected derivational complexity
.

{defi}

[Expected Derivational Complexity,
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}
]
For a relation
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
,
we define its
expected derivational complexity

\operatorname{edc}_{\to}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
as

\operatorname{edc}_{\to}(n)=\sup\{\operatorname{edh}_{\to}(t)\mid t\in\mathcal{T},|t|\leq n\}
.

Note that as in the non-probabilistic setting,
this definition uses the expected derivation height of a term,
and not the expected derivation length of a rewrite sequence.
Hence, the expected derivational complexity
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}
relates to

\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
more than to
\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
.
Indeed, we may have both
\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
and
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}\in\Theta(\omega)
, see
Counterex.
11
in the next section.
Here,
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}\in\Theta(\omega)

means that there is some
n\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}(n)=\omega
,
and hence,
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}(n^{\prime})=\omega
for all
n^{\prime}\geq n
.
Furthermore, we have the following easy observation regarding
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
,
where
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}\in o(\omega)
means that for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}(n)<\omega
.

Lemma 10

(Relation between
\operatorname{edc}
and
\mathtt{SAST}
)
.

Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
where
\mathcal{T}=\mathcal{T}(\Sigma,\mathcal{V})

for a finite signature
\Sigma
.
Then
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}\in o(\omega)
iff
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
.

Proof 3.1
.

If we do not have
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
, then
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}\in\Theta(\omega)

follows directly from the definition.
On the other hand, if
\Sigma
is finite, then
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}(n)=\sup\{\operatorname{edh}_{\to}(t)\mid t\in\mathcal{T},|t|\leq n\}
where
\{t\in\mathcal{T}\mid|t|\leq n\}
is
finite (up to variable renamings), so that the supremum
is equal to
\operatorname{edh}_{\to}(t)
for a term
t\in\mathcal{T}
with
|t|\leq n
.
Hence, if
\operatorname{edc}_{\to}\in\Theta(\omega)
, then there exists some

n\in\mathbb{N}
and a term
t\in\mathcal{T}
such that
|t|\leq n
and
\operatorname{edh}_{\to}(t)=\omega
, such that
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
does not hold.

Finally, for expected runtime complexity we additionally require basic start terms again.

{defi}

[Expected Runtime Complexity,
\operatorname{erc}_{\to,\mathcal{P}}
]
For a relation
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
, the
expected runtime complexity

\operatorname{erc}_{\to,\mathcal{P}}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{N}\cup\{\omega\}
w.r.t. a PTRS
\mathcal{P}
is

\operatorname{erc}_{\to,\mathcal{P}}(n)=\sup\{\operatorname{edh}_{\to}(t)\mid t\in\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}},|t|\leq n\}
,
where

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
denotes the basic terms w.r.t.
\mathcal{P}
.

When considering the expected runtime complexity
for a rewrite relation like
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
, we again omit the additional index
\mathcal{P}
,
i.e.,
\operatorname{erc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{P}}=\operatorname{erc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

3.3.
Relating Positive and Strong Almost-Sure Termination

We now present novel results on the
relation between
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
Similar to the implication from
\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
to
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}
,
it is well known that
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
implies
\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
,
and this implication is again strict
[
3
]
.
While the counterexample for
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}=\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

in
[
3
]
uses infinitely many rules, the following new example
shows that even for PTRSs with
finitely
many rules,

\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
are not equivalent.

Counterexample 11
.

Consider the PTRS
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}
with the rules

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x)

\displaystyle\to\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}(x)),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{b}\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x)

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{g}(x)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{0})

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{0}\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{s}(x))

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{g}_{1}(\mathsf{s}(x))\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}_{1}(\mathsf{s}(x))

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{h}(\mathsf{s}(x))\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}(\mathsf{s}(x))

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{q}(\mathsf{h}(x))\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{h}(\mathsf{0})

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{a}(\mathsf{a}(\mathsf{a}(\mathsf{a}(\mathsf{0}))))\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}(\mathsf{a}(x))

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{q}_{1}(x)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}_{1}(x)

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{q}_{2}(x)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{q}_{2}(x)

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{a}(\mathsf{a}(\mathsf{a}(\mathsf{a}(\mathsf{q}(x)))))\}

and the following rewrite sequence:

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}))&\to_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}^{2}&\mathsf{h}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}))&\to_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}^{n}&\mathsf{q}^{n}(\mathsf{h}(\mathsf{0}))\\
&\to_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}&\mathsf{q}^{n}(\mathsf{a}^{4}(\mathsf{0}))&\to_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}^{3*4}&\mathsf{q}^{n-1}(\mathsf{a}^{4^{2}}(\mathsf{q}(\mathsf{0})))\\
&\to_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}^{3*4^{2}}&\mathsf{q}^{n-2}(\mathsf{a}^{4^{3}}(\mathsf{q}^{2}(\mathsf{0})))&\to_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}^{3*4^{3}}&\ldots\\
&\to_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}^{3*4^{n-1}}&\mathsf{q}(\mathsf{a}^{4^{n}}(\mathsf{q}^{n-1}(\mathsf{0})))&\to_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}^{3*4^{n}}&\mathsf{a}^{4^{n+1}}(\mathsf{q}^{n}(\mathsf{0}))\end{array}

To ease readability, here we wrote
\to_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}
instead of

\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}
and we
omitted the multi-distributions, as all the used rules have trivial probabilities,
i.e., they are of the form
\ell\to\{1:r\}
for some
\ell,r\in\mathcal{T}
.
Thus, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n>0
, the term
\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}))
has an expected
derivation height of

2+n+1+3*\sum_{i=1}^{n}4^{i}=n+3*\sum_{i=0}^{n}4^{i}=n+3*\tfrac{4^{n+1}-1}{3}=4^{n+1}-1+n
.

Next, consider the possible derivations of
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
.
If we never use the rule from
\mathsf{f}
to
\mathsf{g}
, then the expected derivation length is

\tfrac{1}{2}\cdot 1+\tfrac{1}{4}\cdot 2+\tfrac{1}{8}\cdot 3+\ldots=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tfrac{i}{2^{i}}=2

and if we never use the probabilistic
\mathsf{f}
-rule, then the derivation length is
2
as well.
Otherwise, if we use the probabilistic

\mathsf{f}
-rule in the first
k\in\mathbb{N}
steps with
k>0
and the rule from
\mathsf{f}
to
\mathsf{g}
in the
(k+1)
-th step, then
the expected derivation length is

(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tfrac{i}{2^{i}})+\tfrac{1}{2^{k}}+\tfrac{4^{k+1}-1+k}{2^{k}}=(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tfrac{i}{2^{i}})+2^{k+2}+\frac{k}{2^{k}}
.
Thus, for every rewrite sequence starting with
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
, the expected derivation length is finite.
However, since
k
can be any number, for any
k>0
, there is a rewrite sequence starting with

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
whose expected derivation
length is
(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\tfrac{i}{2^{i}})+2^{k+2}+\frac{k}{2^{k}}\geq 2^{k+2}
.
In other words, although every rewrite sequence starting with

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
has finite expected derivation length, the supremum over the lengths
for all these rewrite sequences is infinite.
Thus, we do not have
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}
,
as
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
has infinite expected derivation height,
but
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}
holds,
as every rewrite sequence starting with

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
has finite expected derivation length
and a similar argument holds for every other start term.

The PTRS in Counterex.
11
is of a very specific form:
Its signature contains only unary symbols and constants,
and the probabilistic rule
\mathsf{f}(x)\to\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}(x)),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{b}\}
is erasing.
If we remove one of these properties, then

\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}
does not hold either.

{exa}

Reconsider
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}
from Counterex.
11
.
If we extend the signature by a binary symbol
\mathsf{c}
, then

\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}
does not hold anymore, as we can start with the term
\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}))
and consider the following rewrite sequence.

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\{1:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}))\}\\
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}&\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}))}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0})),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}))\}\\
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}&\{\hskip 34.14322pt\ldots\hskip 34.14322pt,\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}:\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}))}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}^{2}(\mathsf{0})),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}))\}\\
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}&\{\hskip 34.14322pt\ldots\hskip 34.14322pt,\hskip 34.14322pt\ldots\hskip 34.14322pt,\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{b},\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}))}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}^{3}(\mathsf{0})),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}))\}\\
\end{array}

Here, we use the term
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
in
\mathsf{c}
’s first argument
to create infinitely many copies
of the term
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
in
\mathsf{c}
’s second argument
using the probabilistic
\mathsf{f}
-rule.
The underlined terms are not yet normal forms, as they contain the subterm
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
, and will be evaluated further.
As in Counterex.
11
, for each
k>0
,
there is a rewrite sequence starting with

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
whose expected derivation length is at least
2^{k+2}
.
We can choose
k=1
for the first underlined term,
k=2
for the second, etc.,
leading to a final expected derivation length of at least

\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\tfrac{1}{2^{i}}\cdot 2^{i+2}=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}4
, which diverges to infinity.
Hence,
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}
does not hold
over the extended signature.
In particular, this shows that in contrast to ordinary termination for non-probabilistic TRSs,

\mathtt{PAST}
is not preserved under extensions of the signature.

Similarly, if we consider
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}^{{}^{\prime}}
with the non-erasing rule

\mathsf{f}(x)\to\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}(x)),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{b}(x)\}
using
a unary function symbol
\mathsf{b}

instead of
\mathsf{f}(x)\to\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}(x)),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{b}\}
, then we
obtain
the following rewrite sequence:

\begin{array}[]{ll}&\{1:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}))\}\\
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}&\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{b}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0}))}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})))\}\\
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}&\{\hskip 28.45274pt\ldots\hskip 28.45274pt,\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}:\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{b}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})))}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}^{2}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})))\}\\
\mathrel{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}\!\!_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}&\{\hskip 28.45274pt\ldots\hskip 28.45274pt,\hskip 34.14322pt\ldots\hskip 34.14322pt,\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{b}(\mathsf{s}^{2}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})))}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{8}}:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}^{3}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})))\}\\
\end{array}

Again, we can extend this sequence to an infinite rewrite sequence with an infinite expected derivation length.
Hence,
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}
does not hold either.

Counterex.
11
and
Section

3.3

show that
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
is not preserved under signature extensions for any strategy
s\in\mathbb{S}
,
as the first rewrite sequence given in
Section

3.3
is in fact a leftmost-innermost rewrite sequence.

Theorem 12

(Signature Extensions for
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
)
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{S}
.
There exists a PTRS
\mathcal{P}
and signatures
\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}
with
\Sigma\subset\Sigma^{\prime}

such that
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
holds over the signature
\Sigma
,
but not over
\Sigma^{\prime}
.

In contrast,
when analyzing modularity of
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

in
Section

7
, we will show
that they are closed under signature extensions (see
Theorem

48
).

The core idea of both examples in
Section

3.3

is that in the limit we can reach an infinite multi-distribution
whose support contains an infinite number of
terms like
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})
with unbounded expected derivation height.
A PTRS that allows such sequences is said to
admit infinite splits
.

{defi}

[Infinite Splits]
A PTRS
\mathcal{P}

admits infinite splits

if for every term
t\in\mathcal{T}
there exists a term
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}

and an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST whose root is labeled with

(1:t^{\prime})
such that there are infinitely many leaves labeled with
terms that have
t
as a subterm.

{exa}

\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}
over the signature containing a function symbol of arity
\geq 2

and the non-erasing PTRS
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}^{{}^{\prime}}
both admit infinite splits.
However,
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}
over the signature

\{\mathsf{f},\mathsf{b},\mathsf{s},\mathsf{0},\mathsf{g},\mathsf{g}_{1},\mathsf{h},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{q},\mathsf{q}_{1},\mathsf{q}_{2}\}

does not admit infinite splits.

As shown by the following theorem,
admitting infinite splits implies
that
