-rewrite sequence

\vec{\nu}=(\nu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that starts with a basic term
such that

(i)

\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}|_{\mathcal{P}}\geq\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}|\nu_{n}|_{\mathcal{P}}

(ii)

\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\mu})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\nu})

The requirement of
basic start terms is a real restriction for orthogonal PTRSs,
i.e., there exist very simple orthogonal PTRSs
\mathcal{P}
, where
\mathtt{PSN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
starting in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
holds,
but not
\mathtt{PSN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
in general.
One can see this already in the non-probabilistic setting for the TRS
\mathcal{R}
consisting of
the two rules
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}))\to\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}))
and
\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{b})\to\mathsf{b}
,
where we do not have
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
, but
\mathtt{SN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
starting in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
for every
s\in\mathbb{S}
.

The following example shows that
Theorem

33
does not hold for arbitrary start terms.

Counterexample 35
.

Consider the PTRS
\mathcal{P}_{9}
with the two rules:

\displaystyle\mathsf{g}

\displaystyle\to\{\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}:\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{g}),\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}:\mathsf{0}\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}(x))

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(x),\mathsf{f}(x))\}

This PTRS behaves similarly to
\mathcal{P}_{1}
(see Counterex.
17
).
We do not have
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}}
(and hence, also
neither
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}}
nor
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}}
),
as we have
\{1:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g})\}\rightrightarrows_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}^{2}\{\nicefrac{{3}}{{4}}:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g})),\nicefrac{{1}}{{4}}:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})\}
,
which corresponds to a random walk biased towards non-termination (since
\tfrac{3}{4}>\tfrac{1}{4}
).

However, the only basic terms for this PTRS are
\mathsf{g}
and
\mathsf{f}(t)
for terms
t
that
do not contain
\mathsf{g}
or
\mathsf{f}
.
A sequence starting with
\mathsf{g}
corresponds to flipping a biased coin and a sequence
starting with
\mathsf{f}(t)
will clearly terminate.
Hence, we have
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}}
(and thus, also

\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}}
and
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}}
)
starting in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
.
Furthermore, note that we have
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}}
(and thus, also

\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}}
and
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}}
) for arbitrary start terms,
analogous to
\mathcal{P}_{1}
.
Since
\mathcal{P}_{9}
is OR and SP, this shows that
Theorem

33
cannot be extended to
\mathtt{PSN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
in general.

For expected complexity, we obtain a result that is analogous to
Theorem

33
.
Since we require basic start terms, the following theorem
only holds for expected runtime complexity
and not for expected derivation complexity.

Theorem 36

(From Innermost to Full Expected Runtime Complexity)
.

If a PTRS
\mathcal{P}
is OR and SP, then:

\operatorname{erc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}=\operatorname{erc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

Thus, this theorem weakens the requirement of RL in

Theorem

21
to SP (recall that right-linearity
implies spareness).
A corresponding corollary for the non-probabilistic setting would be subsumed by

Theorem

8
,
which ensures
\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}=\operatorname{rc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{R}}}
for all
spare overlay systems
\mathcal{R}
.
As shown by
Section

4.1
, OS is not enough in the probabilistic setting, but one
needs OR.

One may wonder whether
Theorem

33
can nevertheless be used in order
to prove
\mathtt{PSN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for a PTRS
\mathcal{P}
on
all
terms (instead of just
basic start terms) by using a suitable transformation from

\mathcal{P}
to another PTRS
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
such that
\mathtt{PSN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
holds for
all terms iff

\mathtt{PSN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}}
holds when starting with
basic terms.
In
[
38
]
, a transformation was presented
that extends any (non-probabilistic) TRS
\mathcal{R}
by generator rules
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{R})
such that the
derivational complexity of
\mathcal{R}

is the same as the runtime complexity of
\mathcal{R}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{R})
,
where
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{R})
are considered to be
relative
rules whose rewrite steps do not “count” for the complexity.
This transformation can indeed be reused to move from

\mathtt{PSN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

starting in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
to
\mathtt{PSN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
on
arbitrary terms.

The idea of the transformation is to introduce
a new constructor symbol
\mathsf{cons}_{f}
for every defined symbol
f\in\Sigma_{D}
,
and to introduce a new defined
symbol
\mathsf{enc}_{f}
for every function symbol
f\in\Sigma
.
As an example for
\mathcal{P}_{8}
from
Section

5.2
,
then instead of starting with the non-basic term
\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}))
,
we start with the basic term
\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{c}}(\mathsf{cons}_{\mathsf{g}},\mathsf{cons}_{\mathsf{f}}(\mathsf{cons}_{\mathsf{g}}))
, its
basic variant
.
The new defined symbol
\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{c}}
is used to first build the term
\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}))

at the beginning of the rewrite sequence, i.e., it converts
all occurrences of
\mathsf{cons}_{f}
for
f\in\Sigma_{D}
back into the defined symbol
f
, and then we can proceed as if we started with the term
\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{g},\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}))
directly.
For this conversion, we need another new defined symbol
\mathsf{argenc}
that iterates through the term and
replaces all new constructors
\mathsf{cons}_{f}
by the original defined symbol
f
.
Thus, we define the generator rules as in
[
38
]

(just with trivial probabilities in the right-hand sides
\ell\to\{1:r\}
),
since we do not need any probabilities during this initial construction of the original start term.

{defi}

[Generator Rules
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})
]
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a PTRS over the signature
\Sigma
.
Its
generator rules

\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})
are the following set of rules

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{enc}_{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\to\{1:f(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{argenc}(x_{k}))\}\mid f\in\Sigma\}

\displaystyle\cup\;

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{argenc}(\mathsf{cons}_{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}))\to\{1:f(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{argenc}(x_{k}))\}\mid f\in\Sigma_{D}\}

\displaystyle\cup\;

\displaystyle\{\mathsf{argenc}(f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}))\to\{1:f(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{argenc}(x_{k}))\}\mid f\in\Sigma_{C}\},

where
x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}
are pairwise different variables and where the function symbols
\mathsf{argenc}
,
\mathsf{cons}_{f}
, and
\mathsf{enc}_{f}
are fresh (i.e., they do not occur in
\mathcal{P}
).
Moreover, we define
\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})}=\{\mathsf{enc}_{f}\mid f\in\Sigma\}\cup\{\mathsf{argenc}\}\cup\{\mathsf{cons}_{f}\mid f\in\Sigma_{D}\}
.

{exa}

For the PTRS
\mathcal{P}_{9}
from Counterex.
35
,
we obtain the following generator rules
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{9})
:

\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{g}}

\to

\{1:\mathsf{g}\}

\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{f}}(x_{1})

\to

\{1:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}))\}

\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{c}}(x_{1},x_{2})

\to

\{1:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}),\mathsf{argenc}(x_{2}))\}

\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{s}}(x_{1})

\to

\{1:\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}))\}

\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{0}}

\to

\{1:\mathsf{0}\}

\mathsf{argenc}(\mathsf{cons}_{\mathsf{g}})

\to

\{1:\mathsf{g}\}

\mathsf{argenc}(\mathsf{cons}_{\mathsf{f}}(x_{1}))

\to

\{1:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}))\}

\mathsf{argenc}(\mathsf{c}(x_{1},x_{2}))

\to

\{1:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}),\mathsf{argenc}(x_{2}))\}

\mathsf{argenc}(\mathsf{s}(x_{1}))

\to

\{1:\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}))\}

\mathsf{argenc}(\mathsf{0})

\to

\{1:\mathsf{0}\}

As mentioned, using the symbols
\mathsf{cons}_{f}
and
\mathsf{enc}_{f}
, as in
[
38
]
every term over

\Sigma
can be transformed into a basic term over
\Sigma\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})}
.

The following lemma shows that by adding the generator rules, one can indeed reduce the
problem of proving
\mathtt{AST}
on all terms to

\mathtt{AST}
starting in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
.

Lemma 37

(From all Terms to Basic Terms (1))
.

For any PTRS
\mathcal{P}
we have:

\displaystyle\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\Longleftrightarrow\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})}}\text{ starting in }\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}.\!

If one extends the definition of
\mathtt{PAST}
by
relative rules, then
similar results should also be possible for
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
,

\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
, and both
expected derivational and runtime
complexity.
When considering the additional generator rules as ordinary instead of relative rewrite rules,
we obtain an upper bound on the expected complexity and sufficient conditions for
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

Lemma 38

(From all Terms to Basic Terms (2))
.

For any PTRS
\mathcal{P}
we have:

\displaystyle\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\Longleftarrow\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})}}\text{ starting in }\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\Longleftarrow\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})}}\text{ starting in }\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}

\operatorname{edc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\leq\operatorname{erc}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})}}

However, even if
\mathcal{P}
is spare, the PTRS

\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})
is not guaranteed to be spare, although the generator rules
themselves are right-linear.
The problem is that the generator rules include a rule like

\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{f}}(x_{1})\to\{1:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}))\}
where
a defined symbol
\mathsf{argenc}
occurs below the duplicating symbol
\mathsf{f}
on the right-hand
side.
Indeed, while
\mathcal{P}_{9}
is spare,

\mathcal{P}_{9}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{9})
is not. For example, when starting with the basic term

\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{f}}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{cons}_{\mathsf{g}}))
, we have

\begin{array}[]{r@{\;\;}l@{\;\;}l}\{1:\mathsf{enc}_{\mathsf{f}}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{cons}_{\mathsf{g}}))\}&\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}_{9})}^{2}&\{1:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{argenc}(\mathsf{cons}_{\mathsf{g}})))\}\\
&\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{9}}&\{1:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{argenc}(\mathsf{cons}_{\mathsf{g}})),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{argenc}(\mathsf{cons}_{\mathsf{g}})))\}\end{array}

where the last step is not spare.
In general,
\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})
is guaranteed to be spare if
\mathcal{P}
is right-linear.
So we could modify
Theorem

33
into a theorem which states that

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
holds for all terms iff
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})}}
holds when starting in

\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
(and thus, for all terms)
for orthogonal and right-linear PTRSs
\mathcal{P}
.
However, this theorem would be subsumed by
Theorem

18
, where we
already showed the equivalence of
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
if
\mathcal{P}
is orthogonal and right-linear. Indeed,
our goal in
Theorem

33
was to find a weaker requirement than right-linearity.
Hence, such a transformational approach to move from
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
on all start terms to
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

starting in
\mathcal{T}_{\mathcal{B}}
does not seem viable for
Theorem

33
.

6.
Implementation and Evaluation

We implemented our new criteria for the equivalence of

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

in our termination prover
AProVE

[
39
]
.
For every PTRS, one can indicate
whether one wants to analyze its termination behavior for all start terms or only for basic start terms.

AProVE
’s main technique for termination analysis of PTRSs
\mathcal{P}
is the probabilistic DP
framework from
[
54
,
52
]
to prove
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
,
and its adaption for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
from
[
56
]
,
which is however strictly less powerful than the framework for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
The general idea of the DP framework is
a
divide-and-conquer
approach where
processors
are used
to replace a termination problem by several new sub-problems that
are easier to analyze than the original problem.
These processors are applied repeatedly
until all sub-problems are solved.
Since different techniques can be used to analyze the different sub-problems,
this results in a
modular
approach for almost-sure termination analysis.

For our evaluation, we developed a
“
new
” version of
AProVE
, where we consider

18
and
33
,
and afterwards apply the DP framework as described above.
More precisely,
if one wants to analyze
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for a PTRS
\mathcal{P}
,
the “
new
” version of
AProVE
first tries to prove that
the conditions of
Theorem

18
are satisfied if one
regards arbitrary start terms or that
the conditions of
Theorem

33
are satisfied if one only wants to consider
basic start terms.
If this succeeds, then it uses the probabilistic DP framework for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
,
which then implies
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
6
6

6

Currently, we only use the switch from full to innermost rewriting as a preprocessing step
before applying the DP framework.
As shown in
[
56
]
,
a corresponding modular processor
within
the DP framework
(which requires the criteria of our theorems
not for the whole PTRS, but just for specific sub-problems
within the termination proof) would be unsound.

If these theorems cannot be applied, then
AProVE

tries to prove
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
via the DP framework for

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

[
56
]
, or using a direct application of polynomial orderings
[
54
]
.
So in contrast to the experiments in our conference paper

[
53
]
, where we only used the
direct application of polynomial orderings to prove
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
, in this case, “
new
” now
also uses the DP framework for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
from
[
56
]
.

We compare “
new
” with the
“
old
” version of
AProVE
that does not apply any of the theorems of
the current paper. Thus, it directly uses the DP framework for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
from
[
56
]

or the direct application of polynomial orderings
[
54
]
.

Note that for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
w.r.t. basic start terms,

Theorem

33

generalizes
Theorem

18
, since right-linearity implies
spareness.
To show the advantage of
Theorem

33

compared to
Theorem

18
, we also experimented with the variant
“
old
+
Theorem

18
” where we always used

Theorem

18
instead of

Theorem

33
, even when only proving

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for basic start terms.

We used the benchmark set of 130 PTRSs from
[
56
]
,
where
AProVE
can prove
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for 109 of them.

Table

1
shows for how many of these 130 PTRSs the respective strategy
allows
AProVE
to conclude
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

old

old
+
Theorem

18

new

Arbitrary Terms

52

57

57

Basic Terms

59

65

70

Table 1
.
Results

The increase in the number of solved examples from “
old
” to “
new
”
shows that even with dedicated strategies and techniques like the DP framework
for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

[
56
]
,
it is still beneficial to use the techniques from the current paper to move from

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
to
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
whenever possible, since

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
is significantly easier to prove than
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
Moreover, the table shows that the weakening of right-linearity to spareness (i.e., the
generalization of
Theorem

18
to
Theorem

33
) indeed
increases power when considering basic start terms.
As shown in the experiments of
[
66
]
, our

18
and
33
are also very useful in order to move from

\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
vs.
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
, i.e., they often allow us to use the DP framework for

\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and expected innermost complexity analysis from
[
66
]

in order to prove
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and to analyze expected runtime complexity for full rewriting.

For details on our experiments, our collection of examples, and for instructions on how to run our implementation
in
AProVE
via its
web interface
or locally, we refer to:

https://aprove-developers.github.io/InnermostToFullAST/

7.
Modularity

In this section, we investigate the modularity of probabilistic notions of termination.
We will see that as in the non-probabilistic setting
[
43
]
,
in contrast to innermost probabilistic rewriting,
full
probabilistic rewriting is not modular in general.
However, our results on the relation between innermost and full probabilistic
rewriting from the previous sections will allow us to obtain
modularity results for full probabilistic rewriting as well.
A property is called
modular
if it is preserved for certain unions of PTRSs.
Modularity is not only interesting from a theoretical point of view, but in practice it is
also very important to know
whether one can split a huge PTRS into smaller parts
such that the property of interest is preserved.
Then, one can analyze these parts independently of each other.
As remarked earlier, this is also the main idea of the DP framework,
and in fact, we already benefit from
similar modularity results within the DP framework itself.
The fewer conditions are required for modularity of a notion of termination,
the stronger the corresponding DP framework is expected to be.

We will study two different forms of
unions, namely
disjoint unions
(
Section

7.1
), where both PTRSs
do not share any function symbols, and
shared constructor unions

of PTRSs
(
Section

7.2
),
which may have common constructor symbols, but whose defined symbols are
disjoint.
7
7

7

The dependency pair
framework for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

in
AProVE
is
already capable of
splitting
disjoint unions and shared constructor unions into sub-problems that can
be analyzed independently.
However, in this section our goal is
to analyze the modularity of different
probabilistic notions of termination in general. Thus, this allows the use of these
modularity results also outside the DP framework.

In future work, one may extend this analysis to
hierarchical unions
,
which are unions of PTRSs where the first PTRS may
contain defined symbols
of the second one, but not vice versa. Moreover, in this section we only consider the strategies

s\in\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{i}\}
(but it is not difficult to show
that our modularity results for innermost rewriting also hold for
leftmost-innermost rewriting).

In
Section

7.3
, we will also investigate signature extensions.
We have already seen in
Theorem

12
that
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
is not closed under signature extensions for any
s\in\mathbb{S}
.
Based on our modularity results, we will now
show that both
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
are closed under signature extensions.

To distinguish the function symbols of different
PTRSs
\mathcal{P}
,
in the following we write
\Sigma_{D}^{\mathcal{P}}
,
\Sigma_{C}^{\mathcal{P}}
, and
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}

for the defined symbols, constructor symbols, and all
function symbols occurring in the rules of
\mathcal{P}
, respectively.

7.1.
Disjoint Unions

We first consider unions of systems that do not share any function symbols, i.e.,
we consider two PTRSs
\mathcal{P}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
such that
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cap\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}=\varnothing
.
In the non-probabilistic setting,

[
43
]
showed that innermost termination is modular for disjoint unions.
This result can be lifted to
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
We first investigate
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
, as illustrated by the following example.

{exa}

Consider the PTRS
\mathcal{P}_{10}=\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(1)}:\mathsf{f}(x)

\displaystyle\to\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{f}(x),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{a}\}\!

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}:\mathsf{g}(x)

\displaystyle\to\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{g}(x),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{b}\}\!

\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}
both correspond to a fair coin flip,
where one terminates when obtaining heads.
Hence, for both systems we have
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(1)}}}
and
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}}}
,
and thus also
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(1)}}}
and
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}}}
.
Furthermore,
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(1)}}\cap\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}}=\varnothing
,
i.e.,
\mathcal{P}_{10}
is a disjoint union.
When reducing a term like

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(x))
which
contains symbols from both systems,
then we first reduce the innermost redex
\mathsf{g}(x)
until we reach a normal form.
Due to the innermost strategy, we cannot rewrite at the position of
\mathsf{f}
beforehand.
This reduction only uses one of the two systems, namely
\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}
, hence it
converges with probability
1
.
Then, we use the next innermost redex, which will be
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})
,
using only rules of
\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(1)}
until we reach a normal form,
where symbols from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}}
do not influence the reduction.
The reason is that all subterms below or at positions of symbols from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}}

are in normal form, and there is no symbol from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}}
above the

\mathsf{f}
at the root position.
Again, this reduction converges with probability
1
.
Thus, in the end, our reduction starting with
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(x))
also converges with probability
1
,
and the same holds for arbitrary start terms, which implies
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{10}}}
.

In
Section

7.1
, we considered the term

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(x))

where we swap once
between a symbol
\mathsf{f}
from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(1)}}
and a symbol
\mathsf{g}
from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}_{10}^{(2)}}
on the path
from the root to the “leaf”
of the term.
In the proof of
Theorem

39

we lift the argumentation of

Section

7.1

to arbitrary terms via induction.
This proof idea was also used by
[
43
]
in the non-probabilistic
setting to show
the modularity of innermost termination for disjoint unions.

Theorem 39

(Modularity of
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for Disjoint Unions)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
be PTRSs with

\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cap\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}=\varnothing
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}}\;\text{ and }\;\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}\!

Proof 7.1

(Proof Sketch)
.

The direction “
\Longrightarrow
” is trivial and thus, we only prove
“
\Longleftarrow
”. Assume that we have
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}}
and
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}
.

For
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}
, it suffices to regard only rewrite sequences that start
with multi-distributions of the form
\{1:t\}
(see

Lemma

49
in App.
A
for a proof).
Thus, we show by
structural induction on the term structure that for every

t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cup\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}},\mathcal{V})
, all

\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
-rewrite sequences starting with
\{1:t\}

converge with probability 1.

If
t\in\mathcal{V}
, then
t
is in normal form.
If
t
is a constant, then w.l.o.g. let
t\in\mathcal{P}^{(1)}
.
Since we have
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}}
,

t
cannot start an infinite
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
-rewrite sequence
that converges with probability
<1
.

In the induction step
we have
t=f(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})
.
Due to the innermost evaluation strategy, we can only rewrite at the root position
if every proper subterm is in normal form. Thus, we first only consider rewrite steps
below the root.
By the induction hypothesis, every
infinite
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
-rewrite sequence that starts with some
\{1:q_{i}\}

converges with probability
1
, and hence, every
infinite
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
-rewrite sequence that starts with

\{1:t\}
converges with probability
1
as well if it does not
perform rewrite steps at the root position.
However, in the probabilistic setting, this observation
requires a quite complex approximation of the convergence probability.
The reason is that if we rewrite a term
q_{i}
to
\{p_{1}:q_{i,1},\ldots,p_{m}:q_{i,m}\}
, then we obtain
a distribution
\{p_{1}:f(q_{1},\ldots,q_{i,1},\ldots,q_{k}),\ldots,p_{m}:f(q_{1},\ldots,q_{i,m},\ldots,q_{k})\}
.
Now, the terms
q_{j}
with
j\neq i
occur multiple times in this distribution, and we may use different rules to rewrite them.
Hence, the order in which we rewrite the different
q_{i}
matters and cannot be
chosen arbitrarily (as seen in Counterex.
24
).
This
duplication of the terms
q_{j}

is also the reason why
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
is not modular for disjoint systems, see
Section

3.3
.

In the second step, we also allow rewrite steps at the root position. W.l.o.g., let the root symbol

f
of
t
be from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
.
Before performing a rewrite step at the root,
we can replace all maximal (i.e., topmost)
subterms of
t
with root symbols from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}

by fresh variables (using the same variable for the same subterm).
8
8

8

Note that this
replacement is only performed in the induction step at the root,
and not for every swap between symbols from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
and
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}

on the path from the root to the leaves of
t
.

Since
f
is the root symbol (i.e., there
is no symbol at a position above
f
),
and all proper subterms are in normal form,
this replacement does not change the convergence probability. After the replacement,
we only have function symbols from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
, and thus the convergence
probability of the resulting rewrite sequence is 1 since
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}}
holds.

In contrast to
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
,

\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
cannot be modular due to the potential extension of the
signature (recall that by
Theorem

12
,
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
is not closed under
signature extensions).

Counterexample 40
.

Consider the PTRS
\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}
from Counterex.
11
,
and a PTRS
\mathcal{P}_{11}
containing a binary function symbol
\mathsf{c}
such that

\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{11}}}
and
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}\cap\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}_{11}}=\varnothing
hold.
For example,
\mathcal{P}_{11}
could consist of
the only rule
\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{d},\mathsf{d})\to\{1:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{e},\mathsf{e})\}
.
As explained in
Section

3.3
, due to the signature extension by a
binary function symbol, we do not have
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}\cup\mathcal{P}_{11}}}
,
while both
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathsf{unary}}}}
and
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{11}}}
hold.

Finally, we consider
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
To prove that

\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
is modular for
disjoint unions,
we have to show that
the expected derivation height of any term
t
is finite.
However, after rewriting
t
’s proper subterms to normal forms, as we did in
Section

7.1

and in the induction proof of
Theorem

39
,
we may end up with infinitely many different terms. All their expected derivation heights have
to be considered in order to compute
the expected derivation height of
t
.

{exa}

Consider the PTRSs
\mathcal{P}_{12}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}_{12}^{(2)}
with

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{12}^{(1)}:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}(x),y)

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{f}(x,y)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(x,\mathsf{s}(y))

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{f}(x,y)\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{a}

\displaystyle\to\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{0},\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{a})\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{12}^{(2)}:\mathsf{g}(x)

\displaystyle\to\{1:x\}

Clearly, we have both
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{12}^{(1)}}}
and

\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{12}^{(2)}}}
.
Now consider the term
t=\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}))
.
Due to the innermost strategy, we have to
rewrite its proper subterms first. When proceeding in a similar way as in the
induction proof of
Theorem

39
,
then one
would first construct bounds on the expected derivation heights of the proper subterms,
and then use them to obtain a bound on the expected derivation height of the whole
term
t
.
However, reducing
t
’s proper subterms can create
infinitely many different terms,
i.e., all terms of the form
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}),\mathsf{s}^{m}(\mathsf{0}))
for any
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
can
be reached with a certain probability.
Since there is no finite supremum on the derivation height of

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}),\mathsf{s}^{m}(\mathsf{0}))
for all
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
,
one would have to take the individual probabilities for reaching
the terms
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}),\mathsf{s}^{m}(\mathsf{0}))
into account in order to prove that the expected
derivation height of
t
is indeed finite.

Instead, we use an easier argument to show
that any term like
t
has finite expected derivation height.
Recall that in the induction proof of
Theorem

39
, in the induction step we first
rewrite below the root position until every proper subterm is in normal form. Afterwards, if the root
symbol of
t
is from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
, then we replace all maximal subterms of
t

with root symbols from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
by fresh variables. This results in a
term
t^{\prime}
over
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
which is considered for the remaining derivation.
As shown in
Section

7.1
, there may be infinitely many such
terms
t^{\prime}
, e.g., in
Section

7.1
,
t^{\prime}
can be
any term of the form
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{s}^{n}(\mathsf{0}),\mathsf{s}^{m}(\mathsf{0}))
. However, this infinite set of terms
can be over-approximated using the following
finite abstraction.

For the root symbol
\mathsf{f}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
of
t=\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}))
, the normal
forms reachable from
\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a})
can be over-approximated by considering the
normal forms reachable from the
argument
\mathsf{a}
of
\mathsf{g}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
(because function symbols like
\mathsf{g}
may have “collapsing
rules” which return their arguments) or
by fresh variables (which represent possible normal forms
that start with symbols from

\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
). Thus, instead of considering the
rewrite steps at symbols
from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}

in
t
, instead we can consider all rewrite steps for the terms from the multiset
\{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(x,\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},y),\mathsf{f}(x,y)\}
. This multiset is called
the
disjoint union abstraction
of
t
for
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
.
Note that all terms in this disjoint union abstraction are indeed from
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}},\mathcal{V})
.
To also capture the possibility that the two occurrences of

\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a})
in
t
might reach the same normal form that starts with a symbol from

\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
, the disjoint union abstraction of
t
also contains
\mathsf{f}(x,x)
where
we identify the variables
x
and
y
.

Similarly, instead of considering the rewrite steps at symbols
from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
in
t
, we consider the two arguments of
\mathsf{f}
(i.e., the two
occurrences of
\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a})
)
with roots from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}

where each occurrence of the subterm
\mathsf{a}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
is replaced by a fresh variable
(to represent possible normal forms that start with symbols from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
).
Thus, the disjoint union abstraction of
t
for
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
contains
\mathsf{g}(x)
and
\mathsf{g}(y)
.

So instead of an induction proof as for the modularity of

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
,
9
9

9

The proof idea
that we use for the modularity of
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for disjoint unions could also have been
used to prove the modularity of
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for disjoint unions
(
Theorem

39
). However, for
Theorem

39
we used an
induction proof instead to have it similar to the
original proof of
[
43
]
in the non-probabilistic setting. Moreover, such an induction
proof will also be required when showing the modularity of
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

for shared constructor unions (
Theorem

46
).

for the modularity proof of
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
,
we replace the start term
t
by all terms in the
disjoint union abstractions for both

\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
and
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
. This is a finite multiset of
K
terms for some

K\in\mathbb{N}
. Since every term in this abstraction is either from

\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}},\mathcal{V})
or from

\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}},\mathcal{V})
, they all have a finite expected derivation height. Hence, if
C_{\max}\in\mathbb{N}

is the maximal expected derivation height of all these terms, then
K\cdot C_{\max}
is a
(finite) bound on the expected derivation height of
t
.

The following definition introduces the
disjoint union abstraction
formally. Here,

\operatorname{A}_{1}(t)
is the multiset where all topmost subterms of
t
with root from

\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
are replaced by fresh variables or by
the abstractions of their subterms. The multiset
\operatorname{Abs}_{1}(t)
results from

\operatorname{A}_{1}(t)
by identifying any possible combination of the variables in the terms of
\operatorname{A}_{1}(t)
.

{defi}

[Disjoint Union Abstraction]
Let
\mathcal{P}^{(1)},\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
be PTRSs with
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cap\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}=\varnothing
.
For any
d\in\{1,2\}
and any
t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cup\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}},\mathcal{V})
,

\operatorname{A}_{d}(t)
and
\operatorname{Abs}_{d}(t)
are multisets of terms from

\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(d)}},\mathcal{V})
, which are defined as follows.

\begin{array}[]{ll}\operatorname{A}_{d}(y)&=\{x\}\text{, if }y\in\mathcal{V},\text{ where $x$ is always a new fresh variable }\\
\operatorname{A}_{d}(f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}))&=\{f(q_{1},\ldots,q_{k})\mid q_{1}\in\operatorname{A}_{d}(t_{1}),\ldots,q_{k}\in\operatorname{A}_{d}(t_{k})\}\text{, if }f\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(d)}}\\
\operatorname{A}_{d}(f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}))&=\{x\}\cup\operatorname{A}_{d}(t_{1})\cup\ldots\cup\operatorname{A}_{d}(t_{k})\text{, otherwise, where
$x$ is always a new fresh variable }\end{array}

So
\operatorname{A}_{d}(t)
is always a linear term, i.e., it never contains multiple occurrences of the
same variable.

For any function
\varphi:X\to X
with
X\subseteq\mathcal{V}
,
let
\sigma_{\varphi}
be the substitution that replaces
every variable
x\in X
by
\varphi(x)

and leaves all other variables unchanged, i.e.,
\sigma_{\varphi}(x)=\varphi(x)
if
x\in X
and
\sigma_{X}(x)=x
otherwise.
Then we define

\operatorname{Abs}_{d}(t)=\{\sigma_{\varphi}(q)\mid q\in\operatorname{A}_{d}(t),\varphi:\mathcal{V}(q)\to\mathcal{V}(q)\}

The
disjoint union abstraction
of
t
is the multiset
\operatorname{Abs}_{1}(t)\cup\operatorname{Abs}_{2}(t)
.

{exa}

Reconsider
\mathcal{P}_{12}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}_{12}^{(2)}
from

Section

7.1
. Here,
we obtain

\begin{array}[]{ll}\operatorname{A}_{1}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a})))&=\{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(x,\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},y),\mathsf{f}(x,y)\}\\
\operatorname{Abs}_{1}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a})))&=\{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(x,\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},y),\mathsf{f}(x,y),\mathsf{f}(y,x),\mathsf{f}(x,x),\mathsf{f}(y,y)\}\\
\operatorname{A}_{2}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a})))&=\{x^{\prime},\mathsf{g}(x),\mathsf{g}(y)\}\\
\operatorname{Abs}_{2}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a})))&=\{x^{\prime},\mathsf{g}(x),\mathsf{g}(y)\}\end{array}

The following lemma states the
two most important properties regarding the disjoint union abstraction.

Lemma 41

(Properties of
\operatorname{Abs}_{d}
)
.

Let

\mathcal{P}^{(1)},\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
be PTRSs with
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cap\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}=\varnothing
,

t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cup\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}},\mathcal{V})
,
and
d\in\{1,2\}
.
Then:

(i)

\operatorname{Abs}_{d}(t)
is finite

(ii)

\operatorname{Abs}_{d}(t)\subseteq\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(d)}},\mathcal{V})

Proof 7.2
.

Simple proof by induction on the term structure.

With the disjoint union abstraction, we can prove the modularity of

\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for disjoint unions.

Theorem 42

(Modularity of
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for Disjoint Unions)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
be PTRSs with

\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cap\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}=\varnothing
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}}\;\text{ and }\;\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}\!

Proof 7.3

(Proof Sketch)
.

The direction “
\Longrightarrow
” is trivial and thus, we only prove “
\Longleftarrow
”.
So let
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
where
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}
.
Let
\mathfrak{T}
be an arbitrary
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST that starts with
(1:t)
.
We show that
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
is bounded by some constant
which does not depend on
\mathfrak{T}
but just on
t
.
This proves that
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
holds.

Since we have
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(d)}}}
,
the expected derivation length of
all
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(d)}}
-RSTs with
d\in\{1,2\}
that start with a term
q\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(d)}},\mathcal{V})

is bounded by some constant
C_{q}<\omega
.
Thus, since
|\operatorname{Abs}_{1}(t)\cup\operatorname{Abs}_{2}(t)|=K\in\mathbb{N}
is finite,
there is a
C_{\max}<\omega
such that for all
q\in\operatorname{Abs}_{1}(t)\cup\operatorname{Abs}_{2}(t)
we have
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{\prime})\leq C_{\max}
for every

\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(d)}}
-RST
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
that starts with
(1:q)
.
Hence, we obtain
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\leq K\cdot C_{\max}
.

{exa}

Let us illustrate the notation in the previous proof sketch by
applying it to the PTRS from

Section

7.1
.
If we reconsider the start term
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}))
,
then
\operatorname{Abs}_{1}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a})))=\{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(x,\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},y),\mathsf{f}(x,y),\mathsf{f}(y,x),\mathsf{f}(x,x),\mathsf{f}(y,y)\}

and
\operatorname{Abs}_{2}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a})))\!=\!\{x^{\prime},\mathsf{g}(x),\mathsf{g}(y)\}
, as in
Section

7.1
.
Moreover, we obtain
C_{\max}=C_{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a})}=2+2+2\cdot\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})^{n+1}\cdot n=2+2+2\cdot 1=6
,
where
C_{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a})}
is the bound on the expected derivation height of the term
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a})
.
(The reason is that reaching a normal form from the subterm
\mathsf{a}
needs
2
steps in expectation,
and then for each generated
\mathsf{s}
we have one additional step,
where each

\mathsf{a}
generates

n

\mathsf{s}
-symbols with probability
(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})^{n+1}
.)
Hence, the overall bound on the expected derivation height for
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}))
is

K\cdot C_{\max}=10\cdot 6=60
.
In fact, the actual expected derivation height for
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{a}))
is
6+2=8

(
2
steps for the two
\mathsf{g}
-symbols, and in expectation
6
steps for the term
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{a})
),
but for the proof any finite bound suffices.

For full rewriting, it is well known that
termination is already not modular in the non-probabilistic setting.

Counterexample 43
.

Reconsider the TRS
\mathcal{R}_{1}
from Counterex.
2.1
.
This TRS is the disjoint union of
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{(1)}=\{\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b},x)\to\mathsf{f}(x,x,x)\}
and

\mathcal{R}_{1}^{(2)}=\{\mathsf{g}\to\mathsf{a},\;\mathsf{g}\to\mathsf{b}\}
.
Both
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}^{(2)}
are terminating, but the disjoint union
\mathcal{R}_{1}
is not.

However, one can reuse our results
from
Section

4
to obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 44

(Modularity of
\mathtt{PSN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for Disjoint Unions)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
be
PTRSs with

\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cap\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}=\varnothing

that are NO and linear. Then we have:

\displaystyle\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}}\;\text{ and }\;\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}

\displaystyle\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}}\;\text{ and }\;\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}\!

7.2.
Shared Constructor Unions

Now we consider unions of PTRSs that may share constructor symbols, i.e.,
we consider two PTRSs
\mathcal{P}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
such that
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}_{D}\cap\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}_{D}=\varnothing
,
called
shared constructor unions
.
Again, we study innermost rewriting first.

In the non-probabilistic setting, innermost termination is also modular for shared
constructor unions
[
43
]
.
However,

\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
was already not modular w.r.t. disjoint unions,
so this also holds for shared constructor unions.
Moreover,
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
also turns out to be not modular anymore for shared
constructor unions.

Counterexample 45
.

Consider the PTRS
\mathcal{P}_{13}=\mathcal{P}_{13}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}_{13}^{(2)}
with the
rules

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{13}^{(1)}:\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{c}(x,y))

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(x),\mathsf{f}(y))\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{0}\}\!

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{13}^{(2)}:\mathsf{g}(x)

\displaystyle\to\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{d}(x)),\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}:x\}

\displaystyle\mathsf{d}(x)

\displaystyle\to\{1:\mathsf{c}(x,x)\}\!

While
\mathcal{P}_{13}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}_{13}^{(2)}
do not have any common defined symbols, they
share the constructor
\mathsf{c}
.
We do not have
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{13}}}

(and thus, not
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{13}}}
),
as the infinite
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{13}}
-rewrite sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}

depicted in the following
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{13}}
-RST has an infinite expected derivation length.

\mu_{0}:
1

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{0}))
\mu_{1}:
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{d}(\mathsf{0})))
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{0})}}
\mu_{2}:
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{0})))
\ldots
\mu_{3}:
(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})^{2}
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{d}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{0}))))
(\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})^{2}
\underline{{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{0}))}}
\mu_{4}:
\ldots
\ldots

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
each underlined term
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{c}^{n}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{0}))

in the tree above can start a reduction of at least length
2^{n}
, where

\mathsf{c}^{n}(\mathsf{0},\mathsf{0})
corresponds to
the full binary tree of height
n

with
\mathsf{c}
in inner nodes and
\mathsf{0}
in the leaves.
Hence, the term
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{0}))
has an expected derivation height of at least
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}\cdot 2^{n}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2}
,
which diverges to infinity.

On the other hand, we have
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{13}^{(1)}}}
,
as
\mathcal{P}_{13}^{(1)}
is a PTRS with only trivial probabilities that corresponds to a terminating TRS.
Moreover,
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}_{13}^{(2)}}}
holds as well, as the
\mathsf{d}
-rule
can increase the number of
\mathsf{c}
-symbols in a term exponentially,
but those
\mathsf{c}
-symbols will never be used.
Thus,
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
is not modular for shared constructor unions.

In contrast to the proof of
Theorem

42
,
we cannot use the disjoint union abstraction anymore to obtain a bound on the expected derivation height,
since symbols from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
can now “generate” constructor symbols of
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
.
However, for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
we can reuse the idea of the previous proof for
Theorem

39

to obtain a similar result for shared constructor unions.

Theorem 46

(Modularity of
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for Shared Constructor Unions)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
be PTRSs with

\Sigma_{D}^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cap\Sigma_{D}^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}=\varnothing
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}}\;\text{ and }\;\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}\!

Proof 7.4
.

The proof for “
\Longleftarrow
”
is again via structural induction on the term
t
in the initial
multi-distribution

\{1:t\}
and very similar to the proof of
Theorem

39
.
The only difference is that in the induction step,
after performing rewrite steps below the root until all proper subterms are in normal form,
if the root is from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
, then
we do not replace all maximal subterms with roots from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}
by fresh variables
but just maximal subterms with root symbols from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}_{D}
, as the constructor
symbols may be used by rules of
\mathcal{P}^{(1)}
.
This, however, does not interfere with the proof idea.

Again, we can reuse our results
from
Section

4
on
the relation between full and innermost rewriting to obtain
the following corollary for full rewriting.
Due to Counterex.
43
, this corollary
does not hold for general PTRSs.

Corollary 47

(Modularity of
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
for Shared Constructor Unions)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}^{(1)}
and
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
be PTRSs with

\Sigma_{D}^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}\cap\Sigma_{D}^{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}=\varnothing

that are NO and linear. Then we have:

\displaystyle\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}}\;\text{ and }\;\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}^{(2)}}}\!

7.3.
Signature Extensions

Finally, we study signature extensions of PTRSs.
While
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
is not closed under signature extensions by
Theorem

12
, we now
consider
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
Signature extensions can be seen as special cases of disjoint unions,
where the second PTRS
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}
contains only trivially terminating rules
over the new signature that we want to add to
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}^{(1)}}
.
Hence, for innermost rewriting,

39
and
42
already imply that
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

are closed under signature extensions.

For full rewriting,

Corollary

44
implies that

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

are closed under signature extensions for non-overlapping and linear PTRSs.
We now show that this also holds for arbitrary PTRSs.
So let
\mathcal{P}
be an arbitrary PTRS over the signature
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}
for the rest of this section.
We consider two cases.

First, let
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}
contain only constants and unary symbols,
e.g.,
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}=\{\mathsf{f},\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}\}
where

\mathsf{f}
is unary and
\mathsf{a},\mathsf{b}
are constants.
If we extend

\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}
by a
signature
\Sigma^{\prime}
that may also contain symbols of other arities,
e.g., a symbol
\mathsf{c}
of arity
2
,
and consider terms from

\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{V})

like
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})))
, then the fresh symbol
\mathsf{c}

“completely separates”
the function symbols
from
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}

occurring below and above
\mathsf{c}
.
More precisely,
instead of
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})))
, it suffices to analyze the start
terms

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})
,
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})
, and
\mathsf{f}(x_{\mathsf{c}})

(where the
\mathsf{c}
-subterm is replaced by a fresh variable
x_{\mathsf{c}}
), which are all from

\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{V})
. The reason is that
rewriting above
\mathsf{c}
does not interfere with the terms below
\mathsf{c}
and vice versa.
Hence, if every rewrite sequence that starts with
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})
,
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})
, or
\mathsf{f}(x_{\mathsf{c}})
converges with probability
1
, then
so does the term
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})))
. In other words, if
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
holds
over the signature
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}
, then
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
also holds
over the signature
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}
.
Furthermore, the expected derivation height of
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})))
is bounded by
3\cdot C_{\max}
,
where
C_{\max}

is the maximum expected derivation height of the terms
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})
,
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})
, and
\mathsf{f}(x_{\mathsf{c}})
,
similar
as in the proof of
Theorem

42
. Thus, if we have

\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
over the signature
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}
, then we also have
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

over the signature
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}
.

Second, we consider the case where

\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}
itself already contains a function symbol
\mathsf{g}

that has at least arity
2
.
Again, the fresh symbols
\mathsf{c}
of
\Sigma^{\prime}
separate the function symbols of

\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}

occurring above and below
them. However, now the expected
derivation height of a term like
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}),\mathsf{f}(y)))

from
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{V})

is not bounded by simply adding the expected derivation heights
of the corresponding terms

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a})
,
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b})
,
\mathsf{f}(y)
,
\mathsf{f}(x_{\mathsf{c}})
from
\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{V})

anymore. The reason is that
\mathcal{P}
might now duplicate subterms (e.g., there could be a
rule like
\mathsf{f}(x)\to\{1:\mathsf{g}(x,x)\}
).
However, for any term
t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{V})
, we can now construct
a term
t^{\prime}\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{V})
over the original signature
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}

that has (at least) the same expected derivation height and (at most) the same convergence probability.

The construction works as follows:
Let
\mathsf{g}\in\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}
be a symbol of arity
2
(if its arity
is greater than two, then we use the term
\mathsf{g}(\_,\,\_,\;x,...,x)
instead, where
x\in\mathcal{V}
).
For example,
if we extend the signature
\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}
by a symbol
\mathsf{c}\in\Sigma^{\prime}
of arity
3
, then
instead of a term like

\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{c}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}),\mathsf{f}(y)))
from

\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime},\mathcal{V})
,
we can consider the term
\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{a}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{f}(\mathsf{b}),\mathsf{g}(\mathsf{f}(y),x_{\mathsf{c}}))))
from

\mathcal{T}(\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}},\mathcal{V})
without the symbol
\mathsf{c}
,
where we do not rewrite the newly added
\mathsf{g}
symbols.
So here, we replaced
\mathsf{c}(\_,\,\_,\,\_)
by
\mathsf{g}(\_,\,\mathsf{g}(\_,\,\mathsf{g}(\_,x_{\mathsf{c}})))
.
Note that this construction works for full but not for innermost rewriting,
since
it may create new redexes with the symbol
\mathsf{g}
that we may have to rewrite when using the
innermost strategy.
However, for innermost rewriting we have already proven closedness
under signature extensions by
39
and
42
, as explained above.

To summarize, this leads to the following theorem.

Theorem 48

(Signature Extensions for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
)
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a PTRS,
s\in\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{i}\}
, and let
\Sigma^{\prime}
be some signature. Then we have:

\displaystyle\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\text{ over }\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\text{ over }\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\text{ over }\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\text{ over }\Sigma^{\mathcal{P}}\cup\Sigma^{\prime}\!

8.
Related Work on Verification of Probabilistic Programs

In the previous sections, we already discussed the connection to related work in term
rewriting.
However, verification of probabilistic programs has also been studied
extensively for imperative programs on numbers,
and for different recursive programming languages like the lambda calculus.
Thus, in this section we discuss existing work on probabilistic
termination analysis outside term rewriting.

Its
hardness
has been investigated in

[
57
,
79
]
,
showing that analyzing almost-sure termination is even more difficult than ordinary
termination when considering the halting problem for given inputs.
In our paper,
\mathtt{SN}
and
\mathtt{AST}
refer to universal termination,
as we consider rewrite sequences starting with arbitrary terms.
While for non-probabilistic programs, universal termination is “harder” than the halting
problem for given inputs, these problems are equally hard for probabilistic programs

[
57
]
.

There exist numerous approaches and
proof rules mainly based on martingales
for different properties of probabilistic programs.
For example, there are techniques for proving
\mathtt{AST}
and
\mathtt{PAST}

[
35
,
23
,
2
,
25
,
46
,
78
,
47
,
24
,
5
,
76
,
27
,
80
]
,
for proving bounds on the termination probability

[
28
,
68
,
26
,
81
,
34
]
, and for
upper and lower bounds on expected runtimes and costs

[
59
,
83
,
33
,
40
,
95
,
10
,
48
,
58
,
77
,
31
,
72
]
.
Many of these approaches can be automated directly
or by extending them with automatic invariant synthesis techniques,
e.g.,
[
61
,
12
,
21
,
62
,
11
]
.
Moreover, one can also use such proof rules within a quantitative program verification infrastructure like
Caesar

[
90
]
.
Some of these proof rules have already been adapted to term rewriting,
e.g., the proof rule of
[
78
]
can be adapted to prove

\mathtt{AST}
of PTRSs via polynomial and matrix interpretations
[
54
]

and it is also used within the probabilistic DP framework
[
54
,
52
,
56
]
.
Further proof rules regarding
\mathtt{PAST}
and
\mathtt{SAST}
have been adapted
in
[
3
]
and again integrated in the corresponding DP framework
[
66
]
.

In particular, there also exist several

tools
to analyze
\mathtt{AST}
,
\mathtt{PAST}
,
and expected costs for imperative probabilistic programs,
e.g.,
Amber

[
76
]
,

KoAT

[
77
,
72
]
,

Eco-Imp

[
10
]
,
Absynth

[
83
]
, and
Pastry

[
86
]
. Moreover,
higher moments for a loop’s variables are analyzed automatically with the tool

Polar

[
81
]
.
These tools mainly consider imperative programs with an innermost evaluation strategy.
Hence, our
results on the
relation between the different rewrite strategies cannot be directly used for
these tools, while our results on modularity may in principle be of interest.
However, our results concern the functional recursive nature of term rewriting,
where one does not have a fixed control flow as in an imperative program.
Our comparison between
\mathtt{PAST}
and
\mathtt{SAST}
in
Theorem

15
should
also hold for
imperative programs which allow for multiple executions in parallel that are considered simultaneously.

Compared to these tools, our implementation in
AProVE
currently can prove
\mathtt{AST}
,

\mathtt{PAST}
,

\mathtt{SAST}
, and analyze the expected complexity of PTRSs.
For algorithms whose termination behavior relies only on numbers,
techniques for imperative programs with built-in support for arithmetic
are usually more powerful than
approaches based on term rewriting. The reason is that for
term rewriting,
numbers have to be represented
via terms, e.g.,
2
can be represented by the term
\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0}))
.
On the other hand,
term rewriting can handle
programs with non-trivial recursive structure and
arbitrary user-defined data structures, as these structures
can easily be represented as terms.
For example, the list
[2,1]
can be represented by the term

\mathsf{cons}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0})),\mathsf{cons}(\mathsf{s}(\mathsf{0}),\mathsf{nil})))
. Thus, tools based on term rewriting
are particularly suitable when
analyzing programs whose
termination depends on, e.g., lists, trees, or graphs.
In the non-probabilistic setting, there are also techniques and tools for term rewriting with
integrated built-in numbers, e.g., for

integer term rewrite systems

[
37
]
or

logically constrained term rewrite systems

[
63
]
.
Lifting these approaches to the probabilistic setting is an interesting direction for future work.

In addition to the related work on probabilistic loop programs, there are also
several approaches for probabilistic recursive programs,
e.g., to analyze the probabilistic lambda calculus
or other higher-order functional languages
based on types or martingales

[
6
,
70
,
20
,
64
,
69
,
88
,
30
]
.
There has also been work on functional languages where
\mathtt{AST}
or
\mathtt{PAST}
is (partly) decidable,
e.g., probabilistic higher-order rewrite schemes

[
60
]
,
probabilistic pushdown automata

[
13
,
18
,
96
]
, and
restricted probabilistic tree-stack automata
[
73
]
.
Many of the results on recursive languages fix a leftmost-innermost rewrite strategy to avoid non-determinism.
Here, our results may be helpful to extend these techniques to different rewrite strategies,
and also our modularity results may be of interest for the different recursive probabilistic languages.

Finally, probabilistic programs that allow for
data structures
are analyzed
in
[
97
,
74
,
17
]
.
While
[
17
]
uses pointers to represent data structures like tables and lists,
[
97
,
74
]
consider a
probabilistic programming language with matching similar to term rewriting
and develop an automatic amortized resource analysis via fixed template potential functions.
However, these latter works are mostly targeted towards specific data structures,
and we consider general term rewrite systems that can model arbitrary data structures.

9.
Conclusion

In this paper, we
presented the first results on the relationship between

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
of a PTRS
\mathcal{P}

for different rewrite strategies
s\in\mathbb{S}
, including several criteria such that

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
implies
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
Our results also hold for
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
,
\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
, and expected complexity,
and all of our criteria are suitable for automation (for spareness,
there exist sufficient conditions that can be checked automatically).
We implemented our criteria for the equivalence of
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
and

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
in our termination prover
AProVE
, and demonstrated their
practical usefulness in an experimental evaluation. Moreover, we developed the first
modularity results for termination of PTRSs under unions and signature extensions.

In the paper,
we already mentioned several topics for future work, e.g.:

•

Improving our current results (in

22
and
28
)
on the relationship between
non-probabilistic innermost and full derivational complexity.

•

Extending our modularity results to hierarchical unions or finding
more specific classes where
