\mathtt{SAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}

is modular for shared constructor unions, or even classes where
\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
becomes modular.

•

Tackling the (hard) problem of adapting Newman’s Lemma
[
85
]

to the probabilistic setting.

Acknowledgements.

We thank Florian Frohn for his help on the earlier conference
paper
[
53
]
and
Stefan Dollase for pointing us to
[
38
]
.

Appendix A
Missing Proofs

In this appendix, we present all missing proofs for
our new contributions and observations.
Most of our proofs use
\to
-RSTs instead of
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequences.
Therefore, in
Section

A.1
we start with the formal definitions
for all required notions via RSTs (where some of them were already mentioned in the main
part
of the paper).
Then, in
Section

A.2
we give the missing proofs for
the theorems and lemmas from

Section

4
and
Section

5
,
concerning the relation between different rewrite strategies.
In
Section

A.3
,
we prove the results regarding modularity and signature extensions from

Section

7
.

A.1.
Characterization via RSTs

We start with the formal definition of RSTs.

{defi}

[Rewrite Sequence Tree (RST)]
Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
.
A
\to
-rewrite sequence tree (
\to
-RST)

\mathfrak{T}
is a labeled tree
\mathfrak{T}\!=\!(N,E,L)
such that

(1)

N\neq\varnothing
is a possibly infinite set of nodes and
E\subseteq N\times N
is a set of directed edges, such that
(N,E)
is a (possibly infinite) directed tree where
vE=\{w\mid(v,w)\in E\}
is finite for every
v\in N
.

(2)

L:N\rightarrow(0,1]\times\mathcal{T}
labels every node
v
by a probability
p_{v}
and a term
t_{v}
.
For the root
v\in N
of the tree, we have
p_{v}=1
.

(3)

For all
v\in N
: If
vE=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{k}\}
, then
t_{v}\to\{\tfrac{p_{w_{1}}}{p_{v}}:t_{w_{1}},\ldots,\tfrac{p_{w_{k}}}{p_{v}}:t_{w_{k}}\}
.

\operatorname{Leaf}
denotes the set of leaves of the
RST and for a node
x\in N
,
d(x)

denotes the depth of node
x
in
\mathfrak{T}
.
Here, the root has depth
0
.
We say that
\mathfrak{T}
is
fully evaluated
if for every
v\in\operatorname{Leaf}

the corresponding term
t_{v}
is a normal form w.r.t.
\to
, i.e.,
t_{v}\in\mathtt{NF}_{\to}
.

Whenever the referenced RST is ambiguous, we will explicitly indicate the corresponding tree.
For instance, for the probability
p_{v}
of the node
v\in N
of some RST
\mathfrak{T}=(N,E,L)
, we may also write
p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}}
, and
N^{\mathfrak{T}}=N
is the set of nodes of the tree
\mathfrak{T}
.

{defi}

[
|\mathfrak{T}|
, Convergence Probability]
Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
.
For any
\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
we define
|\mathfrak{T}|=\sum_{v\in\operatorname{Leaf}}p_{v}

and say that the RST
\mathfrak{T}

converges with probability

|\mathfrak{T}|
.

It is now easy to observe that we have
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}

(i.e., for all
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite
sequences
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}|_{\to}=1
) iff for all

\to
-RSTs
\mathfrak{T}
we have
|\mathfrak{T}|=1
.
To see this, note that every infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that begins
with a single start term (i.e.,
\mu_{0}=\{1:t\}
) can be represented by an

\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
that is fully evaluated such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}|_{\to}=|\mathfrak{T}|
and vice versa. So
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}
holds iff all fully evaluated

\to
-RSTs converge with probability 1.
Furthermore, note that for every
\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
,
there exists a fully evaluated
\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
such that
|\mathfrak{T}|\geq|\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}|
.
To get from
\mathfrak{T}
to
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
we can simply perform arbitrary (possibly infinitely many)
rewrite steps at the leaves that are not in normal form to fully evaluate the tree.

It remains to prove that it suffices to only regard
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequences that start
with a single start term.

Lemma 49

(Single Start Terms Suffice for
{\mathtt{AST}_{\to}}
)
.

Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
.
If
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}
does not hold,
then there exists an infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence

(\mu_{n}^{\prime})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with a single start term,
i.e.,
\mu_{0}^{\prime}=\{1:t\}
, that converges with probability
<1
.

Proof A.1
.

We prove the converse.
Assume that all infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequences

(\mu^{\prime}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with a single start term converge with probability
1
.
We prove that then every infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence converges with probability
1
,
hence we have
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}
.

Let
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be an infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence.
Suppose that we have
\mu_{0}=\{p_{1}:t_{1},\ldots,p_{k}:t_{k}\}
.
Let
(\mu^{(j)}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\mu^{(j)}_{0}=\{1:t_{j}\}
denote the infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence that uses
the same rules
as
(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
does on the term
t_{j}
for every
1\leq j\leq k
.
We obtain

\displaystyle\textstyle\mathrel{\hphantom{=}}\lim_{n\to\infty}|\mu_{n}|_{\to}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{j=1}^{k}p_{j}\cdot|\mu^{(j)}_{n}|_{\to}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\lim_{n\to\infty}p_{j}\cdot|\mu^{(j)}_{n}|_{\to}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}p_{j}\cdot\lim_{n\to\infty}|\mu^{(j)}_{n}|_{\to}

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}p_{j}\cdot 1

\displaystyle\textstyle=\sum_{j=1}^{k}p_{j}=1

We now obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 50

(Characterizing
{\mathtt{AST}_{\to}}
with RSTs)
.

Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
.
Then
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}
holds iff for all
\to
-RSTs

\mathfrak{T}
we have
|\mathfrak{T}|=1
.
This is equivalent to the requirement that for all fully evaluated

\to
-RSTs

\mathfrak{T}
we have
|\mathfrak{T}|=1
.
Moreover,
\mathtt{wAST}_{\to}
holds iff for every term
t\in\mathcal{T}
there exists a fully
evaluated
\to
-RST

\mathfrak{T}
whose root is labeled with
(1:t)
such that
|\mathfrak{T}|=1
.

Next, we recapitulate how
\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
can be formulated in terms of RSTs.

Corollary 51

(Characterizing
{\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}}
with RSTs)
.

Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
, and
\mathfrak{T}
be an
\to
-RST.
By

\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})=\sum_{x\in N\setminus\operatorname{Leaf}}p_{x}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\;\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in N\setminus\operatorname{Leaf}\\
d(x)\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{x}

we define the
expected derivation length
of
\mathfrak{T}
.
We have
\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
iff
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
is finite
for every
\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
.
Similarly,
\mathtt{wPAST}_{\to}
holds iff for every term
t
there exists a fully evaluated

\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}

whose root is labeled with
(1:t)
such that
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
is finite.

Corollary 52

(Characterizing
{\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}}
with RSTs)
.

Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
.
We have
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
iff

\sup\{\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\mid\mathfrak{T}
is an
\to
-RST whose root is labeled with
(1:t)\}

is finite for all
t\in\mathcal{T}
.

Both of these corollaries are again easy to observe, similar to the characterization of
\mathtt{AST}_{\to}

with RSTs in
Corollary

50
.
First note that every infinite
\rightrightarrows
-rewrite sequence
\vec{\mu}=(\mu_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
that begins
with a single start term can be represented by an infinite

\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
that is fully evaluated such that

\operatorname{edl}(\vec{\mu})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-|\mu_{n}|_{\to})=\sum_{x\in N\setminus\operatorname{Leaf}}p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}}=\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
.
The reason is that
whenever we reach a normal form after
n
steps, this is both a normal form in

\mu_{n}
and a leaf at depth
n
of the RST with the same probability.
Otherwise,
if we have a term
t
in
\operatorname{Supp}(\mu_{n})
that is not in normal form,
then there exists a (unique) inner node in
\mathfrak{T}
at depth
n

with the same probability.
Note that we do not need a version of
Lemma

49
for
\mathtt{PAST}_{\to}
and
\mathtt{SAST}_{\to}
,
since both of them already consider single start terms.

A.2.
Proofs for
Section

4
and
Section

5

In this subsection, we present all missing proofs on the relation
between different restricted forms of probabilistic termination.
To this end, we first recapitulate the notion of a
rewrite sequence subtree
from
[
55
]
.

{defi}

[Rewrite Sequence Subtree]
Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
, and let
\mathfrak{T}=(N,E,L)
be an
\to
-RST.
Let
W\subseteq N
be non-empty, weakly connected,
and for all
x\in W
we have
xE\cap W=\varnothing
or
xE\cap W=xE
.
Then, we define the
\to
-
rewrite sequence subtree
(or simply
subtree
)
\mathfrak{T}[W]

by
\mathfrak{T}[W]=(W,E\cap(W\times W),L^{W})
.
Let
w\in W
be the root of
\mathfrak{T}[W]
.
To ensure that the root of our subtree has the probability
1
again,
we use the labeling
L^{W}(x)=(\frac{p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}}}{p_{w}^{\mathfrak{T}}}:t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}})
for all nodes
x\in W
.

The property of being non-empty and weakly connected ensures that the resulting
graph
(W,E\cap(W\times W))
is a tree again.
The property that we either have
xE\cap W=\varnothing
or
xE\cap W=xE
ensures that the sum of
probabilities for the successors of a node
x
is equal to the probability for the node
x
itself.

To prove the theorems in
Section

4
and
Section

5

we only have to prove the corresponding lemmas. Then
the theorems are a direct consequence, similar to the proof of
Theorem

18
given in

Section

4
.
As mentioned earlier, we will always prove the lemmas using rewrite sequence trees.

See
19

Proof A.2
.

Let
\mathcal{P}
be a PTRS that is non-overlapping and linear.
Furthermore, let
\mathfrak{T}
be an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}
-RST.
We create a new
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}
-RST
\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}
such that
|\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}|\leq|\mathfrak{T}|
and
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)})\geq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
.
W.l.o.g., at least one rewrite step in
\mathfrak{T}
is performed at some node
x
with a
redex that is not an innermost redex (otherwise we can use
\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}=\mathfrak{T}
).
The core steps of the proof are the following:

1.

We iteratively move innermost rewrite steps to a higher position in the tree using a construction
\Phi(\_)
.
The limit of this iteration, namely
\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}
, is an
innermost

\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST with
|\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}|\leq|\mathfrak{T}|
. For each
step of the iteration:

1.1

We formally define the construction
\Phi(\_)
that replaces a certain subtree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
by a new subtree
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
, by moving an innermost rewrite step to the root of
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
.

1.2

We show
|\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})|=|\mathfrak{T}_{x}|
.

1.3

We show that
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
is indeed a valid RST.

2.

We show that the same construction also guarantees
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)})\geq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
. For this:

2.1

We prove
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(1)})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(2)})\leq\ldots

2.2

We prove
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)})
.

1. We iteratively move innermost rewrite steps to a higher position.

In
\mathfrak{T}
there exists at least one rewrite step performed at some node
x
, which is not an innermost rewrite step.
Furthermore, we can assume that this is the first such rewrite step in the path from the root to the node
x

and that
x
is a node of minimum depth
10
10

10

If we allowed rules with infinite
support, then there could be infinitely many nodes at minimum depth. Hence, then one
would have to use a more elaborate enumeration of all nodes where a non-innermost step is performed.
with this property.
Let
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
be the subtree that starts at node
x
, i.e.,
\mathfrak{T}_{x}=\mathfrak{T}[xE^{*}]
,
where
xE^{*}
is the set of all reachable nodes (via the edge relation) from
x
.
We then construct a new tree
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
such that
|\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})|=|\mathfrak{T}_{x}|
,
where we use an innermost rewrite step at the root
node
x
instead of the old one,
i.e., we pushed the first non-innermost rewrite step deeper into the tree.
This construction only works because
\mathcal{P}
is non-overlapping and
linear.
11
11

11

For the construction,
non-overlappingness is essential (while
a related construction could also be defined without linearity).
However, linearity is needed to ensure that the probability of termination in the new tree
is not larger than in the original one.

Then, by replacing the subtree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
with the new tree
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
in
\mathfrak{T}
,
we obtain an

\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST
\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}
with
|\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}|=|\mathfrak{T}|
,
where we use an innermost rewrite step at node
x
instead of the old rewrite step, as desired.
We can then do such a replacement iteratively for every use of a non-innermost rewrite step, i.e., we again replace the first non-innermost rewrite step in
\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}
to obtain
\mathfrak{T}^{(2)}
with
|\mathfrak{T}^{(2)}|=|\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}|
, and so on.
In the end, the limit of all these RSTs
\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathfrak{T}^{(i)}
is an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST,
that we denote by
\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}

such that
|\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}|\leq|\mathfrak{T}|
.
So while the termination probability remains the same in each step, it can
decrease in the limit.
12
12

12

As an example, consider a tree
\mathfrak{T}
which is just
a finite path and its path length increases in each iteration by one. Then the limit
\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}
is an infinite path and converges with probability
0
, while
\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{T}^{(1)},\ldots
all converge with probability
1
.

To see that
\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}
is indeed a valid
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST, note that in
every iteration of the construction we turn a non-innermost rewrite step at minimum depth
into an innermost one.
Hence, for every depth
H
of the tree, we eventually turned every non-innermost rewrite step up to
depth
H
into an innermost one. So the construction will not change the tree above depth
H
anymore,
13
13

13

Again, for rules with infinite support, this would not hold anymore. Nevertheless, with a more elaborate enumeration, one should still be able to obtain a valid
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST in the limit.
i.e.,
there exists an
m_{H}
such that
\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}
and
\mathfrak{T}^{(i)}
are the same trees up to depth
H
for all
i\geq m_{H}
.
This means that the sequence
\lim_{i\to\infty}\mathfrak{T}^{(i)}
really converges into an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST.

Next, we want to prove that we have
|\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}|\leq|\mathfrak{T}|
.
By induction on
n
one can prove that
|\mathfrak{T}^{(i)}|=|\mathfrak{T}|
for all
1\leq i\leq n
,
since we have
|\mathfrak{T}^{(i)}|=|\mathfrak{T}^{(i-1)}|
for all
i\geq 2
and
|\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}|=|\mathfrak{T}|
.
Assume for a contradiction that
|\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}|>|\mathfrak{T}|
.
Then there exists a depth
H\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}\!,\,d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}\!(x)\leq H}p_{x}>|\mathfrak{T}|
.
Again, let
m_{H}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}
and
\mathfrak{T}^{(m_{H})}
are the same trees up to depth
H
.
But this would mean that

|\mathfrak{T}^{(m_{H})}|\geq\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(m_{H})}}\!,\,d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(m_{H})}}\!(x)\leq H}p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(m_{H})}}=\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}\!,\,d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}\!(x)\leq H}p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}>|\mathfrak{T}|
, which is a contradiction to

|\mathfrak{T}^{(m_{H})}|=|\mathfrak{T}|
.

1.1 Construction of
\Phi(\_)

It remains to define
the construction
\Phi(\_)
mentioned above.
Let
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
be an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST that performs a non-innermost rewrite step at the root node

x
. This step has the form
t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\{p_{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}:t_{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}},\ldots,p_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}:t_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\}
using the rule

\bar{\ell}\to\{\bar{p}_{1}:\bar{r}_{1},\ldots,\bar{p}_{k}:\bar{r}_{k}\}
, the
substitution
\bar{\sigma}
, and the position
\bar{\pi}
such that
t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\bar{\pi}}=\bar{\ell}\bar{\sigma}
.
Then we have
t_{y_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\bar{r}_{j}\bar{\sigma}]_{\bar{\pi}}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
.

\mathbf{f}
x
\mathbf{i}
\mathbf{i}
\mathbf{i}

Figure 5
.
Tree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
in the Construction of
\Phi(\_)

Instead of applying a non-innermost rewrite step at the root
x

we want to directly apply an innermost rewrite step.
Let
\tau
be the position of some innermost redex in
t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}
below
\bar{\pi}
. The
construction creates a new
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})=(N^{\prime},E^{\prime},L^{\prime})
whose root is
labeled with
(1:t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}})
such that
|\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})|=|\mathfrak{T}_{x}|
, and that directly performs the first rewrite step at position

\tau
in the original tree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}

(which is an innermost rewrite step) at the root of the tree, by pushing it
from the original nodes in the tree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
to the root of the new tree
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
.
(It could also be that this innermost redex was never reduced in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
.)
This can be seen in the diagram in
Figure

5
, which depicts the tree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
.
The boxes represent
i
nnermost rewrite steps at position
\tau
and the dashed lines indicate that we
push this rewrite step to the root.
This push only results in the same convergence
probability due to our
restriction that
\mathcal{P}
is linear.
However, since we are allowed to rewrite above
\tau
in the original tree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
, the actual position of the innermost redex that was originally at position
\tau
might change during the application of a rewrite step.
Hence, we recursively define the position
\varphi_{\tau}(v)
that contains precisely this redex
for each node
v
in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
until we rewrite at this position.
Initially, we have
\varphi_{\tau}(x)=\tau
.
Whenever we have defined
\varphi_{\tau}(v)
for some node
v
, and we have

t_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\{p_{w_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}:t_{w_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}},\ldots,p_{w_{m}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}:t_{w_{m}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\}
for the direct successors
vE=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{m}\}
, using the rule
\ell\to\{p_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,p_{m}:r_{m}\}
, the substitution
\sigma
, and the position
\pi
, we do the following:
If
\varphi_{\tau}(v)=\pi
, meaning that we rewrite this innermost redex, then we set
\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})=\bot
for all
1\leq j\leq m
to indicate that we have rewritten the innermost redex.
If we have
\varphi_{\tau}(v)\bot\pi
, meaning that the rewrite step takes place on a
position that is parallel to
\varphi_{\tau}(v)
, then we set

\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})=\varphi_{\tau}(v)
for all
1\leq j\leq m
, as the position of the innermost redex did not change.
Otherwise, we have
\pi<\varphi_{\tau}(v)
(since we cannot rewrite below
\varphi_{\tau}(v)
as it is an innermost redex), and thus there exists a
\chi\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
\pi.\chi=\varphi_{\tau}(v)
.
Since the rules of
\mathcal{P}
are non-overlapping, the redex must be completely “inside”
the used substitution
\sigma
, and we can find a position
\alpha_{q}
of a variable
q
in
\ell
and another position
\beta
such that
\chi=\alpha_{q}.\beta
.
Furthermore, since the rule is linear,
q
only occurs once in
\ell
and at most once in
r_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
.
If
q
occurs in
r_{j}
at a position
\rho_{q}^{j}
, then we set
\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})=\rho_{q}^{j}.\beta
.
Otherwise, we set
\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})=\top
to indicate that the innermost redex was erased during the computation.
Finally, if
\varphi_{\tau}(v)\in\{\bot,\top\}
, then we set
\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})=\varphi_{\tau}(v)
for all
1\leq j\leq m
as well.
So to summarize,
\varphi_{\tau}(v)
is
now either the position of the innermost redex in
t_{v}
,
\top
to indicate that the redex was erased, or
\bot
to indicate that we have rewritten the redex.

In

.
We now define the
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})

whose root
\hat{v}
is labeled with
(1:t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}})
and
that directly performs the rewrite step
t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=t_{\hat{v}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P},\tau}\{\hat{p}_{1}:t_{1.x}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})},\ldots,\hat{p}_{h}:t_{h.x}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\}
,
with the rule
\hat{\ell}\to\{\hat{p}_{1}:\hat{r}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{h}:\hat{r}_{h}\}\in\mathcal{P}
, a substitution
\hat{\sigma}
,
and the position
\tau
, at the new root
\hat{v}
. Here, we wrote “
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P},\tau}
” to
make the position of the used redex explicit.
We have
t_{\hat{v}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}|_{\tau}=\hat{\ell}\hat{\sigma}
.
Let
Z
be the set of all nodes
z
such that
\varphi_{\tau}(z)\neq\bot
,
i.e., at node
z
we did not yet perform the rewrite step with the innermost redex.
In the example we have
Z=\{v_{0},\ldots,v_{6}\}\setminus\{v_{4}\}
.
For each of these nodes
z\in Z
and each
1\leq e\leq h
,
we create a new node
e.z\in N^{\prime}
with edges as in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
for the nodes in
Z
, e.g., for the node
1.v_{3}
we create edges to
1.v_{5}
and
1.v_{6}
.
Furthermore, we add the edges from the new root
\hat{v}
to the nodes
e.{x}
for all
1\leq e\leq h
.
Note that
x
was the root in the tree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
and has to be contained in
Z
.
For example, for the node
\hat{v}
we create an edge to
1.v_{0}
.
We define the labeling of the nodes in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
as follows
for all nodes
z
in
Z
:

(T-1)
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}
if
\varphi_{\tau}(z)\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}
if
\varphi_{\tau}(z)=\top

(T-2)
p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot\hat{p}_{e}

Now, for a leaf
e.z^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}
either
z^{\prime}\in N
is also a leaf (e.g., node
v_{2}
in our example)
or we rewrite the innermost redex at
position
\varphi_{\tau}(z^{\prime})
at node
z^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
(e.g., node
v_{1}
in our example).
In the latter case, due to non-overlappingness, the same rule

\hat{\ell}\to\{\hat{p}_{1}:\hat{r}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{h}:\hat{r}_{h}\}
and
the same substitution
\hat{\sigma}
were used.
If we rewrite
t_{z^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P},\varphi_{\tau}(z^{\prime})}\{\hat{p}_{1}:t_{w^{\prime}_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}},\ldots,\hat{p}_{h}:t_{w^{\prime}_{h}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\}
,
then we have
t_{w^{\prime}_{e}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=t_{z^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z^{\prime})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scriptsize(T-1)}}}{{=}}t_{e.z^{\prime}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}

and
p_{w^{\prime}_{e}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scriptsize(T-2)}}}{{=}}p_{z^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot\hat{p}_{e}=p_{e.z^{\prime}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
.
Thus, we can copy the rest of this subtree of
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
to
our newly generated tree
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
.
In our example,
v_{1}
has the only successor
v_{4}
, hence we can copy the subtree starting at node
v_{4}
, which is only the node itself, to the node
1.v_{1}
in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
.
For
v_{5}
, we have the only successor
v_{7}
, hence we can copy the subtree starting at node
v_{7}
, which is the node itself together with its successor
v_{9}
, to the node
1.v_{5}
in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
.
So essentially, we just had to define how to construct

\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
for
the part of the tree before we reach nodes
v
with
\varphi_{\tau}(v)=\bot
in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
.
Now we have to show that
|\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})|=|\mathfrak{T}_{x}|

and that
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
is indeed a valid
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST
(i.e., that the edges between nodes
e.z
with
z\in Z
and its successors correspond to rewrite steps with
\mathcal{P}
).

1.2 We show
|\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})|=|\mathfrak{T}_{x}|
.

Let
v
be a leaf in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
.
If
v=e^{\prime}.z
for some node
z\in Z
that is a leaf in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
(e.g., node
1.v_{2}
), then also
e.z
must be a leaf in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
for every
1\leq e\leq h
.
Here, we get
\sum_{1\leq e\leq h}p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(T-2)}}}{{=}}\sum_{1\leq e\leq h}p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot\hat{p}_{e}=p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot\sum_{1\leq e\leq h}\hat{p}_{e}=p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot 1=p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}
, and thus

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e.z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\\
z\in Z,z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\end{subarray}}p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\\
z\in Z\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{1\leq e\leq h}p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\\
z\in Z\end{subarray}}\!\!p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}

If
v=e.z
for some node
z\in Z
that is not a leaf in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
(e.g., node
1.v_{1}
), then we know by construction
that the
e
-th successor
w_{e}
of
z
in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
is not contained in
Z
and is a leaf of
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
.
Let
(zE)_{e}
denote the
e
-th successor of
z
.
Here, we get
p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(T-2)}}}{{=}}p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot\hat{p}_{e}=p_{w_{e}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}
, and thus

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e.z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\\
z\in Z,z\notin\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\end{subarray}}p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{e}\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\\
w_{e}=(zE)_{e},w_{e}\not\in Z,z\in Z\end{subarray}}p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{e}\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\\
w_{e}=(zE)_{e},w_{e}\not\in Z,z\in Z\end{subarray}}p_{w_{e}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}

Finally, if
v
does not have the form
v=e.z
, then
v
is also a leaf in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}

with
p_{v}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}
and for both
v
and its predecessor
u
we have
v,u\not\in Z
, and thus

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\\
v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\end{subarray}}p_{v}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\\
v\in uE,u\not\in Z\end{subarray}}p_{v}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\\
v\in uE,u\not\in Z\end{subarray}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}

Note that these cases cover each leaf of
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
exactly once.
These three equations imply:

\displaystyle|\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})|

\displaystyle=\sum_{z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}}p_{z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e.z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\\
z\in Z,z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\end{subarray}}p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}e.z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\\
z\in Z,z\notin\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\end{subarray}}p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\\
v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\end{subarray}}p_{v}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\\
z\in Z\end{subarray}}\!\!p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}w_{e}\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\\
w_{e}=(zE)_{e},w_{e}\not\in Z,z\in Z\end{subarray}}p_{w_{e}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\\
v\in uE,u\not\in Z\end{subarray}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=\sum_{z\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}}p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=|\mathfrak{T}_{x}|\!

1.3 We show that
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
is indeed a valid RST.

Finally, we prove that
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
is a valid
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST.
Here, we only need to show that
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\{p_{e.{w_{1}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}:t_{e.{w_{1}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})},\ldots,p_{e.{w_{m}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}:t_{e.{w_{m}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\}
for all
z\in Z
as all the other edges and labelings were already present in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
, which is a valid
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST, and we have already seen that we have a valid innermost rewrite step at the new root
\hat{v}
.

Let
z\in Z
.
In the following, we distinguish between two different cases for a rewrite step at a
node
z
of
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
:

(A)

We rewrite at a position
parallel to
\varphi_{\tau}(z)
or we have
\varphi_{\tau}(z)=\top
.

(B)

We rewrite at a position above
\varphi_{\tau}(z)
.
Here, we need that
\mathcal{P}
is NO and linear.

Let
t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\{p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot p_{1}:t_{w_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}},\ldots,p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot p_{m}:t_{w_{m}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\}
,
with a rule
\ell\to\{p_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,p_{m}:r_{m}\}\in\mathcal{P}
,
a substitution
\sigma
, and a position
\pi
such that
t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\pi}=\ell\sigma
.
We have
t_{w_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
.

(A)
We start with the case where
we have
\pi\bot\varphi_{\tau}(z)
or
\varphi_{\tau}(z)=\top
.
By (T-1), we get
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}
if
\varphi_{\tau}(z)\in\mathbb{N}^{*}

and
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}
if
\varphi_{\tau}(z)=\top
.
In both cases, we can rewrite
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
using the same rule,
the same substitution, and the same position,
as we have
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}|_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}|_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\pi}=\ell\sigma

or directly
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}|_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\pi}=\ell\sigma
.

It remains to show that
t_{e.{w_{j}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
, i.e., that the labeling we defined for
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
corresponds to this rewrite step.
Let
1\leq j\leq m
.
If
\varphi_{\tau}(z)\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, then we have

t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{\tau}(z)\bot\pi}}{{=}}t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}=t_{w_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}=t_{e.{w_{j}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
.
If
\varphi_{\tau}(z)=\top
, then

t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}=t_{w_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=t_{e.{w_{j}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
.
Finally, note that the probabilities of the labeling are correct, as we are using the same rule with the same probabilities.

(B)
If we have
\pi<\varphi_{\tau}(z)
, then there exists a
\chi\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
\pi.\chi=\varphi_{\tau}(z)
.
Since the rules of
\mathcal{P}
are non-overlapping, the redex must be completely “inside”
the used substitution
\sigma
, and we can find a position
\alpha_{q}
of a variable
q
in
\ell
and another position
\beta
such that
\chi=\alpha_{q}.\beta
.
Furthermore, since the rule is linear,

q
only occurs once in
\ell
and at most once in
r_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
.
Let
\rho_{q}^{j}
be the position of
q
in
r_{j}
if it exists.
By (T-1), we get
t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}=t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
.
We can rewrite
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
using the same rule,
the substitution
\sigma^{\prime}
with
\sigma^{\prime}(q)=\sigma(q)[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\beta}
and
\sigma^{\prime}(q^{\prime})=\sigma(q^{\prime})
for all other variables
q^{\prime}\neq q
, and the same position,
as we have
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}|_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}|_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\pi}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\chi}=\ell\sigma^{\prime}
.

It remains to show that
t_{e.{w_{j}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}[r_{j}\sigma^{\prime}]_{\pi}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
, i.e., that the labeling we defined for
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
corresponds to this rewrite step.
Let
1\leq j\leq m
.
If
\rho_{q}^{j}
exists, then we have

t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}[r_{j}\sigma^{\prime}]_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}[r_{j}\sigma^{\prime}]_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\pi.\alpha_{q}.\beta}[r_{j}\sigma^{\prime}]_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\rho_{q}^{j}.\beta}=t_{w_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}=t_{e.{w_{j}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
.
Otherwise, we erase the precise redex and obtain

t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}[r_{j}\sigma^{\prime}]_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\hat{\sigma}]_{\pi.\alpha_{q}.\beta}[r_{j}\sigma^{\prime}]_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}=t_{w_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=t_{e.{w_{j}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
.
Again, the probabilities of the labeling are correct, as we are using the same rule with the same probabilities.

2. Analyzing the expected derivation length of
\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}

Our goal is to show that in the construction of the trees
\mathfrak{T}^{(1)},\mathfrak{T}^{(2)},\ldots
, every leaf
v
of
\mathfrak{T}
is turned into leaves of

\mathfrak{T}^{(1)},\mathfrak{T}^{(2)},\ldots
whose probabilities sum up to
p^{\mathfrak{T}}_{v}
,
and whose depths are greater or equal
than the original depth
d^{\mathfrak{T}}(v)
of
v
in
\mathfrak{T}
.
This implies
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(1)})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(2)})\leq\ldots
From this observation, we then conclude

\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)})
.

2.1 We prove
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(1)})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(2)})\leq\ldots

We start by considering how the leaves of
\mathfrak{T}
correspond to
the leaves of

\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}
.
Let
v
be a leaf in
\mathfrak{T}
.
If
v\not\in xE^{*}
, then
v
is also a leaf in
\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}
, labeled with the same probability, and at the same depth.
Otherwise,
v\in xE^{*}
, which means that either
v\in Z
, or
v\not\in Z
but its
predecessor is in
Z
, or neither
v
nor its predecessor are in
Z
.

If
v\in Z
(like the node
v_{2}
in
Figure

6
), then also
e.v
must be a leaf in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
for every
1\leq e\leq h
.
Here, we again have
\sum_{1\leq e\leq h}p_{e.v}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}
as before.
In addition, we also know that if
e.v
is at depth
m
in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
, then
v
is at depth
m-1
in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
.

If
v\not\in Z
but
v
is the
e
-th successor of a node
z\in Z

(like the node
v_{8}
in
Figure

6
), then
e.z
is a leaf in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
.
Here, we again have
p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}
as before.
In addition, we also know that if
e.z
is at depth
m
in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
, then
v
is at depth
m
in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
.

Finally, if neither
v
nor its predecessor are in
Z

(like the node
v_{9}
in
Figure

6
),
then
v
is also a leaf in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
with
p_{v}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}
and at the same depth.

These cases cover each leaf of
\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}
exactly once and all leaves in

\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
are at a depth greater or equal than the corresponding leaves in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
,
implying that for each leaf
u
in
\mathfrak{T}
we can find a set of leaves
\Omega_{u}^{(1)}
in
\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}

such that
d^{\mathfrak{T}}(u)\leq d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}}(w)
for each
w\in\Omega_{u}^{(1)}
,

\sum_{w\in\Omega_{u}^{(1)}}p^{\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}}_{w}=p^{\mathfrak{T}}_{u}
,
and
\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}}=\biguplus_{u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}\Omega_{u}^{(1)}
.

We can now use the same observation for each leaf
w
in the tree
\mathfrak{T}^{(1)}
in
order to obtain a new set
\Xi_{w}^{(2)}
of leaves in
\mathfrak{T}^{(2)}
and define
\Omega_{u}^{(2)}=\bigcup_{w\in\Omega_{u}^{(1)}}\Xi_{w}^{(2)}
.
Again, we get
d^{\mathfrak{T}}(u)\leq d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(2)}}(w)
for each
w\in\Omega_{u}^{(2)}
,

\sum_{w\in\Omega_{u}^{(2)}}p^{\mathfrak{T}^{(2)}}_{w}=p^{\mathfrak{T}}_{u}
,
and
\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(2)}}=\biguplus_{u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}\Omega_{u}^{(2)}
.
We now do this for each
i\in\mathbb{N}
to define the set
\Omega_{u}^{(i)}
for each leaf
u
in the original tree
\mathfrak{T}
.
Overall, this implies
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(1)})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(2)})\leq\ldots
.

2.2 We prove
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)})\geq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
.

From the construction of the
\Omega_{u}^{(i)}
above, in the end, we obtain

\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}=\biguplus_{u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)},

where
\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}=\bigcap_{i^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}}\bigcup_{i>i^{\prime}}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}=\{w\mid w

is contained in infinitely many
\Omega_{u}^{(i)}\}
.
To see this, let
v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}
.
Remember that for every depth
H
of the tree,
there exists an
m_{H}
such that
\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}
and
\mathfrak{T}^{(i)}
are the same trees up to depth
H
for all
i\geq m_{H}
.
This means that the node
v
must be contained in all trees
\mathfrak{T}^{(m)}
with
m\geq m_{d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}(v)}
,
i.e., it is contained in
\biguplus_{u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}
.
The other direction of the equality follows in the same manner.

Since the probabilities of the leaves in
\mathfrak{T}^{(i)}
always add up to the probability of
the corresponding leaf in
\mathfrak{T}
, we have
\sum_{v\in\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}\leq p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}}
for all
u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}
.
We now show
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)})
by considering the two cases where

\sum_{v\in\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}<p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}}
for some
u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}

and where
\sum_{v\in\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}=p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}}
for all
u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}
.

If we have
\sum_{v\in\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}<p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}}
for some
u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}
, then we obtain

\begin{array}[]{rclcl}1&\geq&|\mathfrak{T}|&=&\sum_{u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}}\\
&>&\sum_{u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}\sum_{v\in\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}&=&\sum_{v\in\biguplus_{u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}\\
&=&\sum_{v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}&=&|\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}|\end{array}

and from
|\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}|<1
we directly get
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)})=\infty
.

Otherwise, we have
\sum_{v\in\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}=p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}}
for all
u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}
, and thus we obtain

\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in N^{\mathfrak{T}}\setminus\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}}(u)=n\end{subarray}}p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}}

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in N\setminus\operatorname{Leaf}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}}(u)=n\end{subarray}}p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}}=1-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\operatorname{Leaf}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}}(u)\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}}})

=

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}}(u)\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}})

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}}=\sum_{v\in\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}})

=

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}}(u)\leq n\end{subarray}}\sum_{v\in\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}})

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\forall v\in\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}:d^{\mathfrak{T}}(u)\leq d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}(v)})

\leq

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-\sum_{u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}(v)\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}})

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}\text{ all disjoint}})

=

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\biguplus_{u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}(v)\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}})

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}=\biguplus_{u\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}\limsup_{i\to\infty}\Omega_{u}^{(i)}})

=

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}(1-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}(v)\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}})

({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in N\setminus\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}(u)=n\end{subarray}}p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}=1-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}(v)\leq n\end{subarray}}p_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}})

=

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in N\setminus\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}\\
d^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}(u)=n\end{subarray}}p_{u}^{\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)}}

=

\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)})

and therefore,
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)})\geq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
.

See
23

Proof A.3
.

We only have to prove the non-trivial direction “
\Longleftarrow
”.
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a PTRS that is non-overlapping, left-linear, and non-erasing.

\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\Longleftarrow\mathtt{wAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
:

Assume for a contradiction that we have
\mathtt{wAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
but not
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
This means that there exists an

\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
such that
|\mathfrak{T}|=c
for some
0\leq c<1
.
Let
t\in\mathcal{T}
such that the root of
\mathfrak{T}
is labeled with
(1:t)
.
Since we have
\mathtt{wAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
, there exists another
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST
\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}=(\tilde{N},\tilde{E},\tilde{L})
such that
|\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}|=1
and the root of
\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}

is also labeled with
(1:t)
.
Hence, in
\mathfrak{T}
at least one rewrite step is performed
at some node
x
that is different to the rewrite step performed in
\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}
.
The core steps of the proof are the same as for the proof of
Lemma

19
.
We iteratively push the rewrite steps that would be performed in
\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}
at node
x
to this node in
\mathfrak{T}
.
Then, the limit of this construction is exactly
\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}
, which would mean that

|\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}|\leq c<1
, which is the desired contradiction.
For this, we have to adjust the construction
\Phi(\_)
.
The rest of the proof remains completely the same.

1.1 Construction of
\Phi(\_)

Let
\mathfrak{T}_{x}=\mathfrak{T}[xE^{*}]
be an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST that performs a rewrite step at position
\zeta
at the root node
x
, i.e.,
t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P},\zeta}\{p_{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}:t_{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}},\ldots,p_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}:t_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\}
using the rule
\bar{\ell}\to\{\bar{p}_{1}:\bar{r}_{1},\ldots,\bar{p}_{k}:\bar{r}_{k}\}
, and the substitution
\bar{\sigma}
such that
t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\zeta}=\bar{\ell}\bar{\sigma}
.
Then
t_{y_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\bar{r}_{j}\bar{\sigma}]_{\zeta}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
.
Furthermore, assume that

\tilde{T}_{x}=\tilde{\mathfrak{T}}[x\tilde{E}^{*}]
rewrites at position
\tau
(using a rule

\hat{\ell}\to\{\hat{p}_{1}:\hat{r}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{h}:\hat{r}_{h}\}\in\mathcal{P}
and
the substitution
\hat{\sigma}
)
with
\tau\neq\zeta
. (Note that
if
\tau=\zeta
, then by non-overlappingness the rewrite step would be the same.)
Instead of applying the rewrite step at position
\zeta
at the root
x

we want to directly apply the rewrite step at position
\tau
.

The construction creates a new RST
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})=(N^{\prime},E^{\prime},L^{\prime})
such that

|\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})|=|\mathfrak{T}_{x}|
,
and that directly performs a rewrite step at position
\tau
at the root of the tree,
by pushing it from the original nodes in the tree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
to the root.
This push only results in the same convergence probability due to our
restriction that
\mathcal{P}
is linear and non-erasing.
We need the non-erasing property now, because
\tau
may be above
\zeta
,
which was not possible in the proof of
Lemma

19
.

Again, since we are allowed to rewrite above
\tau
in the original tree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
, the
actual position of the redex that was originally at position
\tau
might change
during the application of a rewrite step.
Hence, we recursively define the position
\varphi_{\tau}(v)

that contains precisely this redex for each node
v
in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
until we rewrite at this position.
Compared to the proof of
Lemma

19
,
since the rules in
\mathcal{P}
are non-erasing,
we only have
\varphi_{\tau}(v)\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
or
\varphi_{\tau}(v)=\bot
.
The option
\varphi_{\tau}(v)=\top
is not possible anymore.
Initially, we have
\varphi_{\tau}(x)=\tau
.
Whenever we have defined
\varphi_{\tau}(v)
for some node
v
, and we have

t_{v}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\{p_{w_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}:t_{w_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}},\ldots,p_{w_{m}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}:t_{w_{m}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\}

for the direct successors
vE=\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{m}\}
, using the rule
\ell\to\{p_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,p_{m}:r_{m}\}
,
the substitution
\sigma
, and position
\pi
, we do the following:
If
\varphi_{\tau}(v)=\pi
, then we set
\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})=\bot
for all
1\leq j\leq m
to indicate that we have rewritten the redex.
If we have
\varphi_{\tau}(v)\bot\pi
, meaning that the rewrite step takes place parallel to
\varphi_{\tau}(v)
, then we set
\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})=\varphi_{\tau}(v)
for all
1\leq j\leq m
, as the position did not change.
If we have
\varphi_{\tau}(v)<\pi
, then we set
\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})=\varphi_{\tau}(v)
for all
1\leq j\leq m
as well, as the position did not change
either.
If we have
\pi<\varphi_{\tau}(v)
, then there exists a
\chi\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
\pi.\chi=\varphi_{\tau}(v)
.
Since the rules of
\mathcal{P}
are non-overlapping, the redex must be completely “inside” the
used substitution
\sigma
, and we can find a position
\alpha_{q}
of a variable
q
in
\ell
and another position
\beta
such that
\chi=\alpha_{q}.\beta
.
Furthermore, since the rule is linear and non-erasing,

q
only occurs once in
\ell
and once in
r_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
.
Let
\rho_{q}^{j}
be the position of
q
in
r_{j}
.
Here, we set
\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})=\rho_{q}^{j}.\beta
.
Finally, if
\varphi_{\tau}(v)=\bot
, then we set
\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})=\varphi_{\tau}(v)=\bot
for all
1\leq j\leq m
as well.

Again, we now define the
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
whose root is labeled with
(1:t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}})
such that
|\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})|=|\mathfrak{T}_{x}|
,
and that directly performs the rewrite step
t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P},\tau}\{\hat{p}_{1}:t_{1.x}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})},\ldots,\hat{p}_{h}:t_{h.x}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\}
,
with the rule
\hat{\ell}\to\{\hat{p}_{1}:\hat{r}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{h}:\hat{r}_{h}\}\in\mathcal{P}
,
the substitution
\hat{\sigma}
,
and the position
\tau
, at the new root
\hat{v}
.
Here, we have
t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\tau}=\hat{\ell}\hat{\sigma}
.
Let
Z
be the set of all nodes
v
such that
\varphi_{\tau}(v)\neq\bot
.
For each of these nodes
z\in Z
and each
1\leq e\leq h
,
we create a new node
e.z\in N^{\prime}
with edges as in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
for the nodes in
Z
.
Furthermore, we add the edges from the new root
\hat{v}
to the nodes
e.{x}
for all
1\leq e\leq h
.
Remember that
x
was the root in the tree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
and has to be contained in
Z
.
We define the labeling of the nodes in
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
as follows
for all nodes
z
in

Z
:

(T-1)
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\delta]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}
for the substitution
\delta
such that
t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}=\hat{\ell}\delta

(T-2)
p_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot\hat{p}_{e}

Now, for a leaf
e.z^{\prime}\in N^{\prime}
either
z^{\prime}\in N
is also a leaf
or we rewrite at the position
\varphi_{\tau}(z^{\prime})
in node
z^{\prime}
in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
.
If we rewrite

t_{z^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P},\varphi_{\tau}(z^{\prime})}\{p_{w^{\prime}_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}:t_{w^{\prime}_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}},\ldots,p_{w^{\prime}_{h}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}:t_{w^{\prime}_{h}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\}
,
then we have
t_{w^{\prime}_{e}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=t_{z^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\delta]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z^{\prime})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scriptsize(T-1)}}}{{=}}t_{e.z^{\prime}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
for the substitution
\delta
such that
t_{z^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\varphi_{\tau}(z^{\prime})}=\hat{\ell}\delta

and
p_{w^{\prime}_{e}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mbox{\scriptsize(T-2)}}}{{=}}p_{z^{\prime}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot\hat{p}_{e}=p_{e.z^{\prime}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
.
Thus, we can copy the rest of this subtree of
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
in
our newly generated tree
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
.
Again, we just had to define how to construct

\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
for
the part of the tree before we reach the
nodes
v
with
\varphi_{\tau}(v)=\bot
in
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
.
As in the proof of
Lemma

19
, we obtain
|\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})|=|\mathfrak{T}_{x}|
.
We only need to show that
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
is a valid
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST, i.e., that

t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\{p_{e.{w_{1}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}:t_{e.{w_{1}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})},\ldots,p_{e.{w_{m}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}:t_{e.{w_{m}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}\}

for all
z\in Z
as in the proof of
Lemma

19
.

1.3 We show that
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
is indeed a valid RST.

Let
z\in Z
.
In the following, we distinguish three different cases for a rewrite step at node
z
of
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
:

(A)

We rewrite at a position parallel to
\varphi_{\tau}(z)
.

(B)

We rewrite at a position above
\varphi_{\tau}(z)
.

(C)

We rewrite at a position below
\varphi_{\tau}(z)
.

The cases (A) and (B) are analogous to our earlier proof, but in Case (A) we cannot erase the redex anymore.
We only need to look at the new Case (C).

(C)
If we have

t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\{p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot p_{1}:t_{w_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}},\ldots,p_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\cdot p_{m}:t_{w_{m}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}\}
,
then there is a rule
\ell\to\{p_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,p_{m}:r_{m}\}\in\mathcal{P}
,
a substitution
\sigma
, and a position
\pi
with
t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\pi}=\ell\sigma
.
Then
t_{w_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
.
Additionally, we assume that
\varphi_{\tau}(z)<\pi
, and thus there exists a
\chi\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
\pi=\varphi_{\tau}(z).\chi
.
By (T-1), we get
t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\delta]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}=t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
for the substitution
\delta
such that
t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\varphi_{\tau}(v)}=\hat{\ell}\delta
.
Since the rules of
\mathcal{P}
are non-overlapping, including the rule
\hat{\ell}\to\{\hat{p}_{1}:\hat{r}_{1},\ldots,\hat{p}_{h}:\hat{r}_{h}\}
that we use at the root of
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
,
the redex for the current rewrite step must be completely “inside” the substitution
\delta
, and we can find a variable position
\alpha_{q}
of a variable
q
in
\hat{\ell}
and another position
\beta
such that
\chi=\alpha_{q}.\beta
.
Furthermore, since the rule is also linear
and non-erasing,
q
occurs exactly once in
\hat{\ell}
and exactly once in
\hat{r}_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
.
Let
\rho_{q}^{j}
be the position of
q
in
r_{j}
.
We can rewrite
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
using the same rule,
the same substitution, and the same position,
as we have
t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}|_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\delta]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}|_{\pi}=\hat{r}_{e}\delta|_{\chi}=\delta(z)|_{\beta}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}|_{\alpha_{q}}|_{\beta}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\varphi_{\tau}(z).\alpha_{q}.\beta}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\varphi_{\tau}(z).\chi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\pi}=\ell\sigma
.

It remains to show that
t_{e.{w_{j}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}=t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}[r_{j}\sigma^{\prime}]_{\pi}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
, i.e., that the labeling we defined for
\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})
corresponds to this rewrite step.
Let
1\leq j\leq m
.
We have

t_{e.z}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\delta]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\pi}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[\hat{r}_{e}\delta^{\prime}]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}=t_{e.{w_{j}}}^{\Phi(\mathfrak{T}_{x})}
for the substitution
\delta^{\prime}
with
\delta^{\prime}(q)=\delta(q)[r_{j}\sigma]_{\beta}
and
\delta^{\prime}(q^{\prime})=\delta(q^{\prime})
for all other variables
q^{\prime}\neq q
.
With this new substitution, we get
t_{w_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\varphi_{\tau}(w_{j})}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\varphi_{\tau}(z).\alpha_{q}.\beta}|_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}=t_{z}^{\mathfrak{T}_{x}}|_{\varphi_{\tau}(z)}[r_{j}\sigma]_{\alpha_{q}.\beta}=(\hat{\ell}\delta)[r_{j}\sigma]_{\alpha_{q}.\beta}=\hat{\ell}\delta^{\prime}
.
Finally, note that the probabilities of the labeling are correct, as we are using the same rule with the same probabilities in both trees.

\mathtt{PAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}\Longleftarrow\mathtt{wPAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
:

By the same construction as for
\mathtt{wAST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
, we also get
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{(\infty)})\geq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
.

See
26

Proof A.4
.

The idea and the construction of this proof are completely analogous
to the one of
Lemma

19
.
We iteratively move leftmost-innermost rewrite steps to a higher position in the innermost RST.
Hence, the resulting tree is a leftmost
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{li}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST.

The construction of
\Phi(\_)
is also analogous to the one
in
Lemma

19
.
The only difference to the proof of
Lemma

19
is that
the original tree
\mathfrak{T}_{x}
is already an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST.
This means that during our construction only Case (A) can occur,
as we cannot rewrite above a redex in an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST.
And for Case (A), we only need the property of being non-overlapping.

Next, we prove the new results from
Section

5
.

See
34

Proof A.5
.

The proof is completely analogous to the one of
Lemma

19
.
We iteratively move the innermost rewrite steps to a higher position using the construction
\Phi(\_)
.
Note that since
\mathcal{P}
is spare and left-linear,
in the construction of
\Phi(\_)
and in the proof of
Case (B), if the innermost redex is below a redex
\ell\sigma
that is reduced next via a
rule
\ell\to\{p_{1}:r_{1},\ldots,p_{m}:r_{m}\}
, then the innermost redex is completely
“inside” the used substitution
\sigma
, and it corresponds to a variable
q
which
occurs only once in
\ell
and at most once in
r_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq m
,
due to spareness of
\mathcal{P}
and the fact that we started with a basic term.
Hence, we can use the same construction as in
Lemma

19
.

For
Lemma

37
we need some more auxiliary functions from
[
38
]
to
decode a basic term over
\Sigma\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})}
into the original term over
\Sigma
.

{defi}

[Constructor Variant, Basic Variant, Decoded Variant]
Let
\mathcal{P}
be a PTRS over the signature
\Sigma
.
For a term
t\in\mathcal{T}
, we define its
constructor variant

\operatorname{cv}(t)
inductively as follows:

\bullet

\operatorname{cv}(x)=x
for
x\in\mathcal{V}

\bullet

\operatorname{cv}(f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}))=f(\operatorname{cv}(t_{1}),\ldots,\operatorname{cv}(t_{n}))
for
f\in\Sigma_{C}

\bullet

\operatorname{cv}(f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}))=\mathsf{cons}_{f}(\operatorname{cv}(t_{1}),\ldots,\operatorname{cv}(t_{n}))
for
f\in\Sigma_{D}

For a term
t\in\mathcal{T}
with
t=f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
, we define its
basic variant

\operatorname{bv}(f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}))=\mathsf{enc}_{f}(\operatorname{cv}(t_{1}),\ldots,\operatorname{cv}(t_{n}))
.
For a term
t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})},\mathcal{V})
, we define its
decoded variant

\operatorname{dv}(t)\in\mathcal{T}
as follows:

\bullet

\operatorname{dv}(x)=x
for
x\in\mathcal{V}

\bullet

\operatorname{dv}(\mathsf{argenc}(t))=\operatorname{dv}(t)

\bullet

\operatorname{dv}(f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}))=g(\operatorname{dv}(t_{1}),\ldots,\operatorname{dv}(t_{n}))
for
f\in\{g,\mathsf{cons}_{g},\mathsf{enc}_{g}\}
with
g\in\Sigma_{D}

\bullet

\operatorname{dv}(f(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}))=f(\operatorname{dv}(t_{1}),\ldots,\operatorname{dv}(t_{n}))
for
f\in\Sigma_{C}

The only difference to the auxiliary functions from
[
38
]
is that
\operatorname{dv}
now also removes the
\mathsf{argenc}
symbols from a term.
This was handled differently in
[
38
]
in order to ensure

|\!\operatorname{dv}(t)|=|t|
, but this is irrelevant for our proofs regarding
\mathtt{PSN}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.

See
37

Proof A.6
.

“
\Longleftarrow
”

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(N,E,L)
be an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST
whose root is labeled with
(1:t)
for some
t\in\mathcal{T}
.
We construct an
(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}))
-RST
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},E^{\prime},L^{\prime})
whose root is labeled with
(1:\operatorname{bv}(t))
where
|\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}|=|\mathfrak{T}|
and
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{\prime})\geq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
.

In
[
38
]
it was shown that

\{1:\operatorname{bv}(t)\}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{3.4pt}{\tiny$\mathbf{i}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightrightarrows}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})}^{*}\{1:t\delta_{t}\}
.
(PTRSs were not considered in

[
38
]
, but since all the
probabilities in the rules of
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})
are trivial, the proof
in
[
38
]

directly translates to the probabilistic setting.)
Here, for any term
t\in\mathcal{T}
the substitution
\delta_{t}
is defined by
\delta_{t}(x)=\mathsf{argenc}(x)
if
x\in\mathcal{V}(t)
and
\delta_{t}(x)=x
otherwise.
The
(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}))
-RST
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
first performs these innermost rewrite steps to get from
\operatorname{bv}(t)
to
t\delta_{t}
, and then we can mirror the rewrite steps from
\mathfrak{T}
.
To be precise, we use the same underlying tree structure and an adjusted labeling such that
p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}}=p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}
and
t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}}\delta_{t}=t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}
for all
x\in N
.
Since the tree structure and the probabilities are the same, we then obtain
|\mathfrak{T}|=|\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}|
.

t
p_{1}

t_{1}
p_{2}

t_{2}
p_{3}

t_{3}
p_{4}

t_{4}
p_{5}

t_{5}
\ldots
\ldots
\ldots

\operatorname{bv}(t)

“
\implies
”

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(N,E,L)
be an
(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}))
-RST
whose root is labeled with
(1:t)
for some term
t\in\mathcal{T}(\Sigma\cup\Sigma_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})},\mathcal{V})
.
We construct an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},E^{\prime},L^{\prime})
inductively such that for all leaves

x
of
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
during the construction there exists a node
\varphi(x)
of
\mathfrak{T}
such that

t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}=\operatorname{dv}(t_{\varphi(x)}^{\mathfrak{T}})\;\text{ and }\;p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}=p_{\varphi(x)}^{\mathfrak{T}}.

(1)

Here,
\varphi
is injective, i.e., every
leaf
x
of
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
is mapped to a (unique) node
\varphi(x)
of
\mathfrak{T}
.
Furthermore, after this construction, if
x
is still a leaf in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
, then
\varphi(x)
is also a leaf in
\mathfrak{T}
.
Hence, we obtain

|\mathfrak{T}|=\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}}}p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}}\geq\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}}p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}=|\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}|
.
Moreover, we only add rewrite steps in the beginning, so that
\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})\leq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{\prime})
.

We label the root of
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
with
(1:\operatorname{dv}(t))
.
By letting
\varphi
map the root of
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
to the root of
\mathfrak{T}
, the
claim (
1
) is clearly satisfied.
As long as there is still a node
x
in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
such that
\varphi(x)
is not a leaf in
\mathfrak{T}
, we do the following.
If we perform a rewrite step with
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})
at node
\varphi(x)
, i.e.,

t_{\varphi(x)}^{\mathfrak{T}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})}\{1:t_{y}^{\mathfrak{T}}\}
for the only successor

y
of
\varphi(x)
, then we have
\operatorname{dv}(t_{\varphi(x)}^{\mathfrak{T}})=\operatorname{dv}(t_{y}^{\mathfrak{T}})
,
i.e., we do nothing in
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
but simply
change the definition of
\varphi
such that

\varphi(x)
is now
y
.
To see why
\operatorname{dv}(t_{\varphi(x)}^{\mathfrak{T}})=\operatorname{dv}(t_{y}^{\mathfrak{T}})
holds, note that we have
\operatorname{dv}(\ell)=\operatorname{dv}(r)
for all rules
\ell\to\{1:r\}\in\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P})
, since
\operatorname{dv}(\mathsf{enc}_{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}))=f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\operatorname{dv}(f(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{argenc}(x_{n})))
, and analogously
\operatorname{dv}(\mathsf{argenc}(\mathsf{cons}_{f}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})))=f(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\operatorname{dv}(f(\mathsf{argenc}(x_{1}),\ldots,\mathsf{argenc}(x_{n})))
.
This can then be lifted to arbitrary rewrite steps.

Otherwise, let
\varphi(x)E=\{y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}\}
be the set of successors of

\varphi(x)
in
\mathfrak{T}
, and we have
t_{\varphi(x)}^{\mathfrak{T}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\{\tfrac{p_{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}}}{p_{\varphi(x)}^{\mathfrak{T}}}:t_{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}},\ldots,\tfrac{p_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{T}}}{p_{\varphi(x)}^{\mathfrak{T}}}:t_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{T}}\}
.
Since
t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}=\operatorname{dv}(t_{\varphi(x)}^{\mathfrak{T}})
and
p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}=p_{\varphi(x)}^{\mathfrak{T}}
by the induction hypothesis,
then we also have

t_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}\{\tfrac{p_{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}}{p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}}:t_{y_{1}}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}},\ldots,\tfrac{p_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}}{p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}}:t_{y_{k}}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}\}

for terms
t_{y_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}
with
t_{y_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}=\operatorname{dv}(t_{y_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}})
and for

p_{y_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}}=p_{y_{j}}^{\mathfrak{T}}
. Thus, when defining
\varphi(y_{j})=y_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq k
,
(
1
) is satisfied for the new leaves
y_{1},\ldots,y_{k}
of
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
.

See
38

Proof A.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}
be an
\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{f}\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}
-RST
whose root is labeled with
(1:t)
for some
t\in\mathcal{T}
.
The construction from the previous proof yields
an
(\mathcal{P}\cup\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{P}))
-RST
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
whose root is labeled with
(1:\operatorname{bv}(t))

where
|\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}|=|\mathfrak{T}|
.
Since the tree structure and the probabilities are the same,
and we only add rewrite steps in the beginning, we get

\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T}^{\prime})\geq\operatorname{edl}(\mathfrak{T})
.

A.3.
Proofs for
Section

7

We start by proving the
cutting lemma
that is needed to prove our
modularity results for
\mathtt{AST}_{\mathrel{\smash{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle s\>$}}}{{\smash{\rightarrow}}}}}_{\mathcal{P}}}
.
It states that if there exists an
\to
-RST
\mathfrak{T}
that converges with probability
<1

and a partitioning of its inner nodes
into two sets
N_{1}
and
N_{2}
such that
every subtree of
\mathfrak{T}
that only contains inner nodes from
N_{1}
converges with probability
1
,
then we can create a subtree of
\mathfrak{T}
that converges with probability
<1
as well
such that every infinite path contains an infinite number of
N_{2}
nodes
(i.e., it does not contain any
infinite path with eventually only nodes from
N_{1}
).

Lemma 53

(Cutting Lemma)
.

Let
\to\;\subseteq\mathcal{T}\times\operatorname{FDist}(\mathcal{T})
,
and let
\mathfrak{T}
be an
\to
-RST with
|\mathfrak{T}|<1
.
Suppose we can partition its inner nodes into

N_{1}\uplus N_{2}

such that
|\mathfrak{T}_{1}|=1
holds for every subtree
\mathfrak{T}_{1}
of
\mathfrak{T}

which only contains inner nodes from
N_{1}
.
Then there exists a subtree
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{T}
with
|\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}|<1

such that every infinite path of
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}
has an infinite number of nodes from
N_{2}
.

Proof A.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{T}=(N,E,L)
be an
\to
-RST with
|\mathfrak{T}|=c<1
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since we have
0\leq c<1
, there is an
\varepsilon>0
such that
c+\varepsilon<1
.
Remember that the formula for the geometric series is:

\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\tfrac{1}{d}\right)^{n}=\tfrac{1}{d-1},\text{ for all }d\in\mathbb{R}\text{ such that }\tfrac{1}{|d|}<1

Let
d=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+2
. Then we have
\frac{1}{d}=\frac{1}{\frac{1}{\varepsilon}+2}<1
and:

\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}+1<\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}+2\Leftrightarrow\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}+1<d\Leftrightarrow\tfrac{1}{\varepsilon}<d-1\Leftrightarrow\tfrac{1}{d-1}<\varepsilon\Leftrightarrow\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\tfrac{1}{d}\right)^{n}<\varepsilon

(2)

We will now construct a subtree
\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},E^{\prime},L^{\prime})
such that every infinite path has an infinite number of
N_{2}
nodes and such that

|\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}|\leq|\mathfrak{T}|+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\tfrac{1}{d}\right)^{n}

(3)

and then, we finally have

|\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:sum-after-all-cuts}}}{{\leq}}|\mathfrak{T}|+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\tfrac{1}{d}\right)^{n}=c+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\tfrac{1}{d}\right)^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{eq:setting-d}}}{{<}}c+\varepsilon<1

The idea of this construction is that we cut infinite subtrees of pure
N_{1}
nodes as soon as the probability for normal forms is high enough.
In this way, one obtains paths where after finitely many
N_{1}
nodes, there is a
N_{2}
node, or we reach a leaf.

The construction works as follows.
For any node
x\in N
, let
\mathcal{L}_{2}(x)
be the number of
N_{2}
nodes in the path from the root to
x
.
Furthermore, for any set
W\subseteq N
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\mathfrak{L}_{2}(W,k)=\{x\in W\mid\mathcal{L}_{2}(x)\leq k\lor(x\in N_{2}\land\mathcal{L}_{2}(x)\leq k+1)\}
be the set of all nodes in
W
that have at most
k
nodes from
N_{2}
in the path from the root to its predecessor.
So if
x\in\mathfrak{L}_{2}(W,k)
is not in
N_{2}
, then we have at most
k
nodes from
N_{2}
in the
path from the root to
x

and if
x\in\mathfrak{L}_{2}(W,k)
is in
N_{2}
,
then we have at most
k+1
nodes from
N_{2}
in the path from the root to
x
.
We will inductively define a set
U_{k}\subseteq N
such that
U_{k}\subseteq\mathfrak{L}_{2}(N,k)
and then define the subtree as

\mathfrak{T}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{T}[\bigcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}U_{k}]
.

We start by considering the subtree
\mathfrak{T}_{0}=\mathfrak{T}[\mathfrak{L}_{2}(N,0)]
.
This tree only contains inner nodes from
N_{1}
.
While the node set
\mathfrak{L}_{2}(N,0)
itself may contain nodes from
N_{2}
,
they can only occur at the leaves of
\mathfrak{T}_{0}
.
Using the prerequisite of the lemma, we get
|\mathfrak{T}_{0}|=1
.
In
Fig.

7
one can see the different possibilities for
\mathfrak{T}_{0}
.
Either
\mathfrak{T}_{0}
is finite or
\mathfrak{T}_{0}
is infinite.
In the first case, we can add all the nodes to
U_{0}
since there is no infinite path of pure
N_{1}
nodes.
Hence, we define
U_{0}=\mathfrak{L}_{2}(N,0)
.
In the second case, we have to cut the tree at a specific depth once the probability of leaves is high enough.
Let
\operatorname{d}_{0}(y)
be the depth of the node
y
in the tree
\mathfrak{T}_{0}
.
Moreover, let
D_{0}(k)=\{x\in\mathfrak{L}_{2}(N,0)\mid\operatorname{d}_{0}(y)\leq k\}
be the set of nodes in
T_{0}
that have a depth of at most
k
.
Since
|\mathfrak{T}_{0}|=1
and
|\_|
is monotonic w.r.t. the depth of
the tree
\mathfrak{T}_{0}
, we can find an
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

\sum_{x\in\operatorname{Leaf}^{\mathfrak{T}_{0}},d_{0}(x)\leq N_{0}}p_{x}^{\mathfrak{T}_{0}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{d}

We include all nodes from
D_{0}(N_{0})
in
U_{0}
and delete every other node of
\mathfrak{T}_{0}
.
In other words, we cut the tree after depth
N_{0}
.
This cut can be seen in
Fig.

7
, indicated by the dotted line.
We now know that this cut may increase the probability of leaves by at most
\frac{1}{d}
.
Therefore, we define
U_{0}=D_{0}(N_{0})
in this case.

N_{1}
N_{2}
N_{2}
N_{1}
N_{1}
\mathtt{NF}
\mathtt{NF}
\mathtt{NF}
N_{2}
N_{2}
N_{1}
N_{1}

(a)

\mathfrak{T}_{x}
finite

N_{1}
N_{2}
N_{2}

N_{1}
\mathtt{NF}
\mathtt{NF}
N_{1}
N_{2}
